It's Head Lice Season. Here's What to Know
2026-09-09

It's Head Lice Season. Here's What to Know

TIME | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings As children return to school, families have a lot to look forward to: fresh routines, new connections, crisper weather—and a greater risk of head lice. September is National Head Lice Prevention Month in the U.S, coinciding with a time of year when infestations tend to surge. “Head lice can occur throughout the year, but cases are often noticed more frequently in late summer and early fall, when children return to school and resume close-contact activities,” says Dr. April Armstrong, chief of dermatology at the University of California, Los Angeles. Head lice is a common childhood condition, with up to 12 million head lice infestations occurring among children ages 3 to 11 every year in the U.S., according to estimates from the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). The insects don’t spread disease, but they can still cause a great deal of distress, doctors say. “Having head lice is not a sign of poor hygiene, but it can cause stress and anxiety because there is this stigma that it is,” says Dr. Carla Torres Zegarra, a pediatric dermatologist at Children’s Hospital Colorado. “If left untreated, it can also be very bothersome, causing itching and disturbing sleep.” Here’s what to know about the pesky parasites, and how to spot, treat, and prevent them. Head lice, or Pediculus humanus capitis, are tiny, wingless insects that feed on human blood. They are found mostly on the scalp, but sometimes appear on eyebrows and eyelashes. Adult head lice are about the size of a sesame seed and are usually tan to grayish-white in color, according to the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP). Head lice lay and attach their eggs—known as nits—to the base of hair shafts using a glue-like substance. Infested people may have a combination of adult and juvenile head lice, nits, and empty egg casings on their scalp and in their hair. No. There are three different types of lice that feed exclusively on humans: head lice, pubic or “crab” lice, and body lice. Unlike head lice, which have elongated bodies, pubic lice have short and round bodies, resembling crabs. Pubic lice typically spread through sexual contact and are most common in adults. When people have body lice, the insects are found primarily on their clothing and bedding, and sometimes on their bodies. Unlike head lice, body lice is connected to hygiene practices and access to clean clothing, and spread primarily through direct contact with someone who has body lice or with their contaminated clothing or bedding. In the U.S., body lice mostly affects people without access to housing, clean clothes, and regular bathing, CDC says. Since head lice can only crawl and not jump or fly, they typically spread via head-to-head contact. “That’s why it happens more in kids, because they play in close proximity,” Torres Zegarra says. Head lice can also spread through objects that touch the head, such as combs, hats, helmets, and pillowcases. But this type of indirect transmission is much less likely since head lice typically survive for less than a day when away from the scalp, and nits can’t hatch at temperatures cooler than that of the human scalp, AAP says. Getting head lice has nothing to do with personal hygiene or the cleanliness of the environment, and is caused solely by exposure to an infested person. If there’s an outbreak of lice at your child’s school or daycare, that’s not a guarantee they’ll get it. Though head lice frequently spread in schools, research has found that head lice actually have a low contagion rate in classrooms. That’s why the CDC, AAP, National Association of School Nurses and other medical groups don’t recommend keeping children with head lice away from school. An itchy scalp is the most common symptom of head lice, “particularly behind the ears and near the back of the neck,” Armstrong says. “Some people also describe a tickling sensation or the feeling that something is moving in the hair. Scratching may produce redness, small sores, or, occasionally, a secondary bacterial infection.” People can have head lice without symptoms, too. “With a first infestation, itching may not begin for four to six weeks because it takes time for the body to develop an allergic response to louse saliva,” Armstrong says. Examining the hair and scalp under bright light and finding live crawling lice is the best way to diagnose a head lice infestation. Using a magnifying glass or a special lice comb can help with this process, AAP says. “Nits located farther from the scalp are often empty or no longer viable, so their presence alone does not necessarily indicate an active infestation,” Armstrong says. Misdiagnosis of head lice is common. “Many presumed lice and nits submitted by physicians, nurses, teachers, and caregivers to a laboratory for identification were found to be artifacts such as dandruff, hairspray droplets, scabs, dirt, or other insects,” such as those blown by the wind and caught in the hair, AAP says. The CDC recommends that people visit a doctor if they suspect they may have lice. Head lice infestations most commonly occur in preschool- and elementary school-age children and their families. Research suggests that girls may get head lice more often than boys. But the AAP says head lice infestations do not appear to be influenced by hair length, hair type, and frequency of shampooing or brushing. There are several over-the-counter and prescription medications approved by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to treat head lice, including lotions and shampoos that contain the insecticides permethrin or pyrethrin. Treatment of head lice often involves using one of these medications, combined with “careful combing using a fine-toothed lice comb,” Armstrong says. Some of these medications kill only lice, while others target both lice and nits. Depending on the medication, a second application may be needed seven to ten days after the first treatment, Armstrong says. In some cases, head lice can be resistant to these topical medications, AAP says. In those situations, manual removal of lice and nits might be necessary—an option also available to people who want to avoid insecticide-containing products. The AAP says home remedies such as using mayonnaise, melted butter, or olive oil could work to suffocate the lice, though these methods are not FDA approved or recommended, and more research still needs to be done on their effectiveness. A prescription oral medication can also be used. Some internet sites recommend using essential oils and other botanical products to kill head lice, but the AAP says “the safety and efficacy of natural or herbal products are currently not regulated by the FDA, and until more data are available, their use in infants, children, and adolescents should be avoided.” The AAP also stresses that head lice treatment is only recommended for people “in whom diagnosis is highly suspected or proven.” Torres Zegarra says she sometimes sees patients with itchy scalps who developed irritation because they tried head lice treatments despite not having any evidence of lice. “What I end up treating is not lice but the skin irritation caused by multiple treatments that were tried on the scalp,” she says. Doctors say that cutting or shaving the hair of an infested person isn’t necessary, and it does not always work to get rid of head lice. “Long or very thick hair may require more product and more time to comb thoroughly, but it can be treated successfully without removing the hair,” Armstrong says. “If live lice remain after treatment has been used correctly, a clinician should determine whether the cause is medication resistance, reinfestation, or an incorrect diagnosis before additional treatments are applied.” “There is no completely reliable way to prevent head lice, especially among young children who naturally play in close contact. The most effective step is to limit direct head-to-head or hair-to-hair contact when lice are known to be circulating,” Armstrong says. If someone is confirmed to have head lice, members of their household and other close contacts should be carefully checked for infestation. “Clothing, towels, and bedding used during the two days before treatment can be washed and dried using heat,” Armstrong says. “Combs and brushes can be soaked in hot water. Extensive cleaning, fumigation, and pesticide sprays are unnecessary.” AAP says parents and other caregivers could check children regularly to catch and treat early infestations, and children can be taught not to share personal items such as combs and hats. They shouldn’t refuse to wear helmets because of fear of head lice, however, particularly since the spread of lice through objects is uncommon, AAP says. Ultimately, Torres Zegarra says “there’s no need to panic” when head lice arise. “It’s a nuisance problem but not a health crisis,” she says, adding that children with head lice shouldn’t be quarantined or kept away from school. “No healthy child should lose out on education because of head lice. They should avoid head-to-head contact, but they shouldn’t be shamed or isolated,” Torres Zegarra says.
TIME
12 Questions You’ll Be Glad You Asked Your Grandparents
2026-09-09

12 Questions You’ll Be Glad You Asked Your Grandparents

TIME | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Elizabeth Keating’s students learn about World War II in history books. But when one of them asked her grandmother what it was like to live through the wartime mobilization and bombing in the U.K., the past suddenly felt real. Talking to her grandmother, the student told Keating, felt like “talking to a celebrity.” “It gives us a link with history that’s very personal,” says Keating, a professor emerita of anthropology at the University of Texas at Austin and author of The Essential Questions: Interview Your Family to Uncover Stories and Bridge Generations. Your grandparents might not think of themselves as eyewitnesses to history—or realize that anyone is interested in the ordinary details of their lives. Asking an unexpected question can make the past come alive while helping you discover who they were, and who they still are. These questions can help you get beyond the familiar family stories—and start conversations you’ll be glad you had. Your grandparent has probably changed their mind about more than a few things over the years. Maybe they swore they would never get married or that they’d stay in their hometown forever. Ask what they felt certain about at your age—and what eventually made them reconsider. The answer can make a younger person think, “Oh my God, you were my age,” says Mike O’Krent, founder of LifeStories Alive, a company that preserves family histories on video. It might also be reassuring to discover that your grandparent once shared some of your fears or wrestled with questions you’re confronting right now. Hearing what happened next can open up a larger conversation about how they became the person they are today. “What was your childhood like?” is an enormous question—the conversational equivalent of handing someone a blank book and asking them to fill it. It’s better to zoom in on one specific setting. “The dinner table is a window into an entire family, sometimes an entire childhood,” says Lauren Befus, founder of Memory Lane Jane, a family-biography company that preserves people’s life stories in heirloom books. Ask who sat where, who cooked, and whether everyone ate together. Was dinner loud and chaotic or silent and formal? Did people linger or bolt the moment they finished? Was there always enough food? Could friends drop in? One person told Keating that “there was no talking at the dinner table.” In Keating’s own family, her mother occasionally made the children prepare little reports from the encyclopedia to present during dinner. “We hated it,” she recalls. Whether your grandparent remembers laughter, pinching under the table, or strictly enforced silence, you’ll learn much more than what was on the menu. Every family has at least one expenditure it considers financially unhinged. Perhaps your grandparent’s parents refused to pay someone to repair anything they could conceivably fix themselves. Maybe restaurant meals, store-bought clothes, or long-distance phone calls were treated as scandalous extravagances. The question can reveal what money represented in your grandparent’s childhood home: security, freedom, status, or a source of constant stress. You might also recognize a few financial commandments that have traveled down the family line. Oh, that’s why your mom refuses to pay for shipping or regards full-price cereal as a personal affront! Your grandparent wasn’t always the person reminding everyone to be careful. Once upon a time, they might have sneaked somewhere forbidden, played hooky from Sunday school to take a nap in the cornfields behind the church, concocted an impressive lie, or attempted to conceal a mishap from their parents. Keating particularly likes asking about trouble because it comes with a built-in plot: What happened? How did you respond? Did anyone find out? The story doesn’t need to involve a wild act of rebellion. She’s found that “you can get into trouble with small things, too, that evolve into bigger things.” Either way, you’ll get to meet the mischievous younger person behind the grandparent you know and love. That battered book, recipe card, or piece of jewelry might look unremarkable. Then you hear the story about why it’s still a prized possession. Keating recalls talking to a woman who had grown up poor and dreamed of owning a particular doll. When she received it for her birthday, she treasured it so deeply that she eventually passed it down through her family. “We attach relationships to objects,” Keating says. Eventually, “an object isn’t just a thing anymore.” It evokes a person, place, yearning, or moment someone wants to keep close. If your grandparent still has the item, ask to see it. Their act of holding it again—or simply studying its scratches, faded colors, or fraying edges—might shake loose a story they didn’t even realize they remembered. The answer could introduce you to a teacher, mentor, neighbor, boss, or friend you’ve never heard about—yet whose encouragement altered the course of your family’s history. For O’Krent, it was his third-grade teacher, Mrs. Glaze. One day, despite making him stay inside during recess because he couldn’t keep his mouth shut, she looked him in the eye and said: “You’re going to be somebody important someday.” He never forgot it. An influential person isn’t always who you would expect, O’Krent says, and the best part of the story may emerge when you ask why your grandparent connected with them. What did they see in your grandparent—and how did their belief change what your grandparent saw in themselves? Maybe your grandparent pursued a career their family didn’t approve of, moved somewhere unexpected, ended—or began—a relationship, or took a risk everyone else considered foolish. Ask why people objected. Then borrow O’Krent’s favorite follow-up: “Do you agree with that?” Your grandparent might still insist everyone else was wrong. Or perhaps the decision led directly to something they’d love to forget. “Everybody makes mistakes,” O’Krent says. What matters is “the lesson you learn from it.” Either way, you’ll discover how your grandparent responded when their judgment collided with other people’s expectations—and whether they’d make the same choice again. Résumés are good for recording promotions and degrees. But they don’t capture the time someone took care of an aging parent, raised children through a miserable year, started over after a failure, repaired a relationship, or became the friend who always showed up. The accomplishments you associate with your grandparent might not be the ones that matter most to them. This question “gives people permission to define accomplishment for themselves,” Befus says. You may discover that the chapter of their life they value most is one you barely knew about. There is, apparently, no birthday at which a person wakes up with life completely sorted out. Grandparents still have evolving identities, relationships, hopes, and uncertainties, says Claudia Johnson, a licensed marriage and family therapist in Seattle. Asking what your grandparent is still figuring out communicates: “I don’t only want to know who you were. I’m interested in who you are now,” Johnson says. Maybe they’re learning how to enjoy retirement, trying to understand a changing friendship, or deciding what they want the next few years to look like. Answer the question yourself, too. That reciprocity transforms the interaction from an interview into a real conversation—and reminds both of you that nobody ages out of uncertainty. Not every meaningful question requires rummaging through the past. That’s especially important when you’re talking to someone with dementia, says Cristina Rodriguez, founder of Mind&Melody, a nonprofit that connects musicians with people living with dementia and other neurological conditions. Rodriguez’s grandmother was diagnosed with dementia a few years ago. As her memory changes, Rodriguez has realized that “connection can’t always depend on asking her to recall the past.” During one FaceTime call, when Rodriguez was thinking about having children, she asked her grandmother what she would name a baby girl today. Estefania, she replied, because it sounded strong and powerful. Rodriguez had known her grandmother her entire life—and knew she had raised six children—but had never thought to ask her anything like that. The exchange showed Rodriguez “how much possibility there is in questions that aren’t about remembering at all.” Ask what song should belong to the two of you, or what your perfect day together would look like. These types of questions let you be curious about who your grandparent is right now, including what they imagine and what makes them laugh. After putting your grandparent in the hot seat, turn the interview over to them. What have they always wondered about you? “The grandparent’s not expecting that one,” O’Krent says. That’s exactly why he likes it: The question gives your grandparent some control over where the conversation goes and makes the exchange feel mutual. Once the conversation is flowing, don’t cling too tightly to your prepared questions. O’Krent teaches people an unusual listening trick: “The next thing you say has to include at least one word from the last sentence they say.” Because you don’t know when that last sentence is coming, you have no choice but to pay close attention. It might feel awkward at first, he acknowledges, but it helps the other person feel heard. Plus, the best question is almost always the follow-up you didn’t plan. This question might expose the blank spaces in your grandparent’s own family history. Maybe they know their mother moved across the country alone but never learned what compelled her to go. Perhaps they inherited a house without ever asking why their grandparents chose it—or what brought their parents together in the first place. The answers often involve what Keating calls “huge identity questions”: Where did I come from? Who am I? Why did my family’s life unfold the way it did? Most people know the milestones, she says, but what they really want is “the story behind everything.” Listen closely. Your grandparent might be telling you not only what they regret leaving unanswered, but which parts of their own story they hope you won’t.

How TIME and Statista Determined the World's Best Companies of 2026
2026-09-09

How TIME and Statista Determined the World's Best Companies of 2026

TIME | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The research project "World’s Best Companies 2026" is a comprehensive analysis conducted to identify the top performing companies around the world. The study is based on three primary dimensions: Employee Satisfaction, Revenue Growth and Sustainability Transparency (ESG). The first dimension, Employee Satisfaction, was investigated based on survey data from a large sample of over 200,000 employees from around the world. The evaluation encompassed direct recommendations of verified employees as well as indirect evaluations from industry peers. The second dimension, Revenue Growth, was assessed using data from Statista’s revenue database, which contains company growth data for the last three years. The companies had to meet certain criteria to be considered for the evaluation, including generating a revenue of at least $100 million in 2024 or 2025 depending on when the most recent data was published during the research phase. Additionally, the companies had to demonstrate positive revenue growth in the last three years. Both relative and absolute growth were considered in the evaluation. The third dimension, Sustainability Transparency, was evaluated based on ESG data among standardized KPIs from Statista’s ESG Database and targeted data research. To formulate a comprehensive ESG index, multiple Key Performance Indicators were collected. For the environmental evaluation, this included the 2024 carbon emissions intensity and reduction rate compared to 2022, as well as the Carbon Disclosure Project (CDP) score. The social dimension assessed the share of women on the board of directors and the existence of a human rights policy. The governance dimension evaluated whether a company had a Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) report adhering to the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) guidelines and a compliance or anti-corruption guideline. Once the data was collected and evaluated, it was consolidated and weighted within a scoring model. The scores of all three dimensions were added on an equal percentage basis to form the final ranking score of a maximum of 100 points. The 1,000 companies with the highest score were awarded as World’s Best Companies 2026 by TIME and Statista.

A number of new startups are also taking this opportunity to translate the investment into innovations, sometimes as standalone products or in partnership with other companies, similar to Anduril’s ventures in the U.S. For example, German drone-maker Quantum Systems, which is currently supplying armed forces in Ukraine, announced significant investment from German telecomm giant Deutsche Telekom (no. 7), which owns the T-mobile brand, and also announced a new partnership with Rheinmetall (no. 287) to co-create an anti-drone shield. The Real Legacy of Lonnie Bunch
2026-09-09

The Real Legacy of Lonnie Bunch

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Americans may not realize for many years just how lucky they were that Lonnie G. Bunch III led the Smithsonian Institution from 2019 to 2026. Bunch has announced that he will step down at the end of this year. He insists that the timing is his own, and we should do him the honor of believing him—notwithstanding the immense pressure the administration has applied to the Smithsonian generally and Bunch personally since Donald Trump returned to office last year. Bunch, 73, would likely have chosen to retire earlier if not for his sense of duty—his conviction (which few dispute) that he was the best person to guide the Smithsonian through a turbulent period. The Smithsonian is the world’s largest museum, education, and research complex. It’s also “part of the glue,” as Bunch himself said shortly after taking office, “that holds the country together.” Since his appointment as the Smithsonian’s top official—its 14th secretary and the first historian and Black man to hold the position—in June 2019, Bunch has been the glue holding the Smithsonian together. Through a combination of principled stances and deft political skills, he has kept the sprawling institution on mission and aligned with its public. Chief Justice John Roberts, who serves as the Smithsonian’s chancellor, said in a statement that Bunch has “pursued excellence in the telling of our nation’s story” and that the Smithsonian has “benefited greatly from his wisdom, vision, and leadership.” Bunch is a fighter, but a smart one. He has often struck me as a master of institutional jiu-jitsu—a leader who knows instinctively when to hold the line, when to yield, and how to convert yielding to the advantage of the institution he has done so much to advance and protect. “When you’re Black in America,” he told The New York Times earlier this year, “you’ve got to figure out how to get through, right? You’ve got to figure out how to build allies, when to stand firm, when to bang the table. So in some ways, all of that prepared me for this moment.” Bunch has worked at the Smithsonian since 1978, interrupted by stints at the California African American Museum in the 1980s and the Chicago Historical Society in the early 2000s. His tenure as secretary included the upheavals of 2020 and 2021, when—after the killing of George Floyd and amid the coronavirus pandemic—activists on the left were accusing museums across the country, including the Smithsonian, of being bastions of white privilege, colonialism, and cultural theft. Bunch remained calm and compassionate in the face of these critiques, compromising when appropriate, but never giving in to bullying. It surely helped that his greatest achievement prior to becoming the Smithsonian’s top official was planning, raising funds for, and launching the National Museum of African American History and Culture. The museum, which opened to great fanfare in 2016, has been hugely popular. Under Bunch’s leadership, the museum balanced a forthright, profoundly moving description of slavery and its long aftereffects with exhibits that bear witness to the extraordinary flowering of African American culture, celebrating a panoply of achievements by Black Americans across many fields of human endeavor. Read: How a museum reckons with Black pain The Smithsonian was founded by an act of Congress in 1846. During the Civil War, it created a controversy by refusing to host a lecture series presented by abolitionists. The institution’s leadership eventually relented, but Frederick Douglass, the most prominent abolitionist of the day, was still not permitted to speak. At the 2019 ceremony installing him as secretary, Bunch referred back to those days: “Today we are here speaking in a place as an African American, where Frederick Douglass could not speak, but we are a different institution.” Bunch’s leadership, and the Smithsonian’s resilience, has been severely tested since Trump’s return to power. Efforts by the institution to tell what Kim Sajet, the former director of the National Portrait Gallery, described as “a fuller, more American story” and “one that fosters connection, reflection and understanding” came into conflict with White House officials who felt that the “fuller story” being told was overly focused on moral failure, hypocrisy, and oppression. The new administration was quick to target the Smithsonian. Some of what gave Bunch moral authority during the Biden years now threatened to work against him. In March 2025, an executive order noted that the United States had witnessed “a concerted and widespread effort to rewrite our Nation’s history.” The Smithsonian, it stated, had, “in recent years, come under the influence of a divisive, race-centered ideology.” Trump announced that he was firing Sajet. The Smithsonian regents asserted that only Bunch had authority over such personnel decisions, but she resigned anyway. More recently, the administration has ramped up pressure on the Smithsonian, demanding an overhaul of its presentations to rid them of perceived liberal bias and attempting to force its hand with explicit threats, such as pulling funding and erecting warning signs in front of the National Museum of American History. (Although it is independent, the biggest share of the institution’s budget comes from the federal government.) Bunch has had to figure out how to handle these various assaults without allowing the Smithsonian’s mission to be compromised. He has said plenty on the public record—all of it sage and diplomatic, yet consistently firm on questions of principle. I can only imagine what private conversations he has been having on a daily basis. Thanks to the double whammy of COVID and activist pressures, few leaders of any prominent organization have had a fun time since 2020. But Bunch has been the leader of an enormous—and enormously important—institution that’s explicitly in the business of (among other things) telling the American story at a time when the question of how to tell that story has become so fraught and divisive. What to do in such circumstances? Perhaps Americans could all begin by just agreeing that the pandas in the National Zoo (which is part of the Smithsonian) are very, very cute. And then, perhaps, that the Apollo 11 command module in the National Air and Space Museum—the one that carried Neil Armstrong, Michael Collins, and Buzz Aldrin to the moon and back—is really cool. If so, perhaps they can next agree that Bunch, during his tenure at the helm of the Smithsonian, has been the exact combination of resolute leader and compassionate and good-humored public servant that America needed him to be.

A Great Novel Becomes a Bad Show
2026-09-09

A Great Novel Becomes a Bad Show

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
For many readers, the career of the novelist Bret Easton Ellis is the standard for mainstream literary stardom. The biopic writes itself: While still a college undergraduate, Ellis strikes it big with his debut novel, 1985’s Less Than Zero, an unsentimental stenography of disaffected, privileged young adults in swanky Los Angeles. It’s soon adapted into a movie starring Robert Downey Jr. and James Spader, which itself bears little resemblance to the book. Nevertheless, Ellis’s renown skyrockets. By the time he turns 30, he’s seemingly done it all: published multiple best sellers (American Psycho, The Rules of Attraction) filled with materialistic ennui, cheap sex, and lurid death; been mythologized as part of a literary “Brat Pack” alongside writers including Jay McInerney and Tama Janowitz; rubbed elbows with celebrities of the music and film worlds; made lots of money (for a writer, at least); and become addicted to drugs. Regardless of how you felt about his work—and readers had all sorts of opinions—his success couldn’t be denied. “If you were not a supporter of the Brat Pack, you simply had to accept us anyway,” he writes in his 2005 novel, Lunar Park, which is narrated by a semifictional version of himself. Such white-hot notoriety was rare in his time, and it’s rarer now. While a book can become a hit in many ways, you’re only young once—and though we’ve all been told about the virtues of writers who labor away unappreciated until they finally get their moment, it’s hard not to be a little envious of the ones who’re able to enjoy their spoils before knee pain sets in. It didn’t matter that, when I finally read Less Than Zero after college, I thought it was a relic best left in its era—the work of a poser trying too hard to convince readers of his secret depth. Ellis’s early works felt too significant, too settled, for me to really say much about; disputing him would have been like arguing with a redwood tree. His reputation as a hedonistic provocateur made the release of his 2023 novel, The Shards, somewhat of a surprise, at least to me. On its face, The Shards is an autofictional murder mystery starring a teenage version of Ellis who, at the start of his senior year of high school, suspects that a new classmate is responsible for a series of gory killings around the school’s ritzy Sherman Oaks neighborhood. Novel-Ellis is working on an early draft of Less Than Zero, drawing from his own party-heavy lifestyle. The sensationalistic violence and substance-fueled navel-gazing—all of that sounds like a typical Ellis novel. Yet The Shards is not celebratory or defiant. It’s narrated by a much older Ellis, who looks back on his youth with regret and shame—about his closeted homosexuality, about his formative and long-dead teenage friendships—and rips apart his own mythos from the inside. Parts of The Shards read like they could have used a more punitive editor—it’s a little long, and Ellis describes himself as “keyed-up” a few too many times. Yet the book’s hefty length—594 pages in hardcover—is conducive to novel-Ellis’s obsessiveness as he combs through memories for hidden meaning and tries to identify each emotion he felt during this chaotic time period. “The facts from that fall were receding from memory but being seventeen actually became clearer to me emotionally,” he writes. “I realized I had needed this distance of forty years to finally begin writing the book.” Now, he suggests, he can admit that Less Than Zero was indeed an act of mimicry—that he’d copied that book’s numbed affectation from a classmate. The cynicism of his early work had put me off, but here he seemed weary enough to let down his guard and, perhaps, tell a version of the truth. The Shards has now been adapted for FX by Ryan Murphy, whose campy and provocative shows (Glee, American Horror Story, Feud) have helped define TV in the 21st century. In many ways, it’s a natural match. Murphy’s eye is consistently drawn to beauty, and to the machinations and aspirations of the rich. And though his scripts can be plenty outlandish, he knows when a performer should play it straight. The early episodes seem to preserve much of Ellis’s plot. We begin by meeting Bret (played by Igby Rigney), an aspiring novelist and senior at the prestigious Buckley School in Sherman Oaks. Bret has recently started dating Debbie (Hayes Warner), a bottle-blond socialite; along with another couple, Susan (Kaia Gerber) and Thom (Graham Campbell), they constitute a glamorous foursome. Beneath this picturesque front is a more complicated reality. Susan and Thom may look like the perfect prom queen and king, but she’s grown restless in their relationship. And while Bret is quietly in love with Susan, he’s also been hooking up with some of his male classmates and wondering how to take advantage of the fact that Debbie’s father, Terry (Wes Bentley), a well-connected (and seemingly gay) movie producer, appears interested in giving him his big break. These delicate dynamics are unsettled by the appearance of a mysterious new transfer student, Robert (Homer Gere), who looks like he walked right out of an Armani advertisement. Bret is transfixed by his new classmate and is convinced that he’s seen Robert before, at the movies, where he made an indelible impression. But Robert becomes strangely defensive about the encounter, and insists that Bret must have made a mistake. His denials and secretive behavior lead Bret to suspect that Robert is connected to the Trawler, a yet-unidentified serial killer who’s been targeting local teenagers. Bret’s obsession with proving his guilt slowly spins out of control. Gruesome deaths, a pulsing ’80s soundtrack, dramatic conversations about people’s hidden motivations—it should be fun, and it sometimes is. As Robert, Gere conveys an alluring darkness; you can’t take your eyes off him, and neither can the show’s characters. Gerber’s Susan is beautifully nonchalant, a quality that propels her into Robert’s arms. (Gere and Gerber are the respective children of Richard Gere and Cindy Crawford, who used to be married.) And Jordan Roth is ridiculous but memorable as Steven, Terry’s assistant, who seems to be manipulating everyone behind the scenes. Yet Murphy makes a subtle tweak that changes The Shards completely. The novel is told from the first-person perspective, which allows an older Bret to constantly remark on the described action with the benefit of time and perspective. Here, that framing device is snipped entirely. Bret still narrates, but voiceover-Bret is not Bret Easton Ellis, the middle-aged author of American Psycho who’s now looking backwards. It’s just Rigney, who plays young Bret, speaking from a vague near future, and sometimes obnoxiously. “Kids today have it fucking easy,” he sneers, which is new writing. The elision of this metafictional narration is paired with a lot of new plot. Ellis co-wrote the first two episodes before being sidelined in a reported power struggle. (He has not promoted the show on his personal Instagram, nor did he appear at a glitzy launch party in July.) The other episodes depart drastically from the book’s structure. Murphy tacks on stray storylines: a dance competition on an American Bandstand–type local TV show, for instance, and a kidnapping plot involving a foulmouthed classmate who has what seems to be a form of Tourette’s. One episode has the characters monologuing at one another in Shakespearean verse while dressed in costume. The add-ons are tonally confusing and parceled out inconsistently. Some moments are played for show-stopping drama, only to be immediately abandoned. Other confrontations have the taste of corny reality TV, and the ’80s references are often played for laughs. A blunter way of saying this: The new stuff is awful, and a waste of time. The show expands Bret’s outer world at the cost of his inner life (and screen time, as Rigney disappears for long stretches), but that outer world isn’t very interesting. The novel’s emotions have been strip-mined, leaving only the surface tensions of vain teenagers. Many TV adaptations of literature miss the mark, because the visual medium isn’t always well suited to novelistic storytelling, which can be both wide-angled and highly internal. But this misfire bears a particular irony. Since Less Than Zero, Ellis has been trailed by critics saying his work is shallow and trades in shock value. The Shards show unfurls as if all of those put-downs were true, as though the book is little more than a collection of unsettling murders, great outfits, and hot people being mean to one another. Two comments about the making of the show—both from that launch party—reveal something about what went awry. The first comes from Murphy, speaking about what drew him to the book: “I knew the songs, I knew the clothes, I knew everything because I had lived it. It was my time.” The second is from Roth, speculating on the show bringing about a trend revival: “Love Story got us our ’90s nostalgia, and Shards brings us our ’80s nostalgia.” The former comment is Murphy viewing The Shards as his story, not Ellis’s—a tricky approach for a book that’s so explicitly personal. The latter is, I think, deeply cynical about the point of a show like this. Even when The Shards invites us to laugh at its characters, it does so through the prism of “Weren’t the ’80s crazy?” The emotions are blustery, but not intimate; the narration has been edited for cool concision. In the book, novel-Ellis narrates about Robert: “The boy aroused something primal in me that I had never felt before—I wanted him immediately, I needed to be his friend, I had to make contact, I had to see him naked, I had to own him.” In the show, this confessional surge has been drastically pared back, keeping Bret’s messy feelings at a distance. Regret and shame, the novel’s animating sentiments, are not sexy; I’m sure the actors had more fun overindexing on the ’80s of it all. The end result, though, is the transformation of a great novel into a bad show, one that Murphy wants to run for two more seasons. Let’s hope not. Although Ellis is often nitpicky about adaptations of his work—he’s never come around to the wonderfully caustic movie version of American Psycho—he’s right to distance himself from this one. “It was nothing like the book,” Bret narrates in both the show and the novel, after coming out of a screening of The Shining. “I felt cheated.” It’s quietly tragic that the same thing has happened here. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

The Sadness of Being the ‘Last Man’
2026-09-09

The Sadness of Being the ‘Last Man’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Francis Fukuyama never promised us we’d be happy. To be fair to the many readers who thought otherwise, The End of History and the Last Man, his famous 1992 book, did give off a whiff of utopia, trumpeting a post–Cold War world in which liberal democracy had vanquished competing political systems and everyone could lie back on their affordable chaise longues and raise a glass to the expiration of all strife and struggle. “Several” people each week still ask the poor man whether he would like to unwrite that book, he says. Yet Fukuyama insists today that if you had read to the last page—in fact, the last five chapters—you would not have expected serenity. His more sober forecast is right there in the original essay that led to the book: “The end of history will be a very sad time.” Fukuyama has published a new memoir, In the Realm of the Last Man, in which he hopes to shake off a reputation that he clearly can’t stand. He is not a naive Pollyanna, and he never was. By the “end of history,” he did mean that we had arrived at the best of all possible worlds—and he still does. But being the “Last Man,” inhabiting this ending, was always presented as an unstable condition. Fukuyama borrowed the concept from the dark mind of Friedrich Nietzsche, who used it to refer to a person bored out of their senses, someone who lives in a society where there is nothing left to risk their life for. What appeared in his earlier book as an alarming afterthought is now front and center for Fukuyama. He wrote The End of History during a time of American triumphalism, as the Berlin Wall was turned into rubble, but the events of the past three decades—9/11, the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan, Donald Trump—have offered up a different story. Fukuyama knows this. His defensiveness keeps him from fully acknowledging how far he’s had to step back from his own previously boosterish views, but he speaks about liberal democracy, this supposedly happy end state, in much gloomier tones. It now sounds like a scary cul-de-sac at night, where the streetlight is busted, an eerie tension fills the air, and there seems to be no way forward. Purely as a memoir, I should say, this one is disappointing. Fukuyama loses interest in telling his own life story around page 60. The fun and revealing anecdotes are front-loaded. For example, as a young foreign-policy analyst, he kept failing a CIA polygraph test because a question about whether he had ever had sex with a man got him completely flustered (he hadn’t, he hastens to tell us). He writes about his father, a sociologist and committed liberal, who never stopped giving Fukuyama a hard time for his once-conservative political opinions (he did work for Ronald Reagan and the RAND Corporation, after all), telling him he was “brainwashed” by other people. When these more intimate insights taper off, so does any causal connection between Fukuyama’s personal background and his evolving thinking. His grandparents were Japanese immigrants who spent World War II in an internment camp. But this is only briefly mentioned, which left me wondering whether the stories they told him made him more attuned to the vulnerabilities of a pluralistic society. We do get a few chapters about what an unapologetic gearhead he is, a lover of woodworking who can also assemble a computer from scratch. Yet Fukuyama spends most of his time and energy on summarizing the ideas that have animated his work, producing a greatest-hits album of insights. This succession of shifting concepts helps clarify how much the bravado that marked The End of History has seeped out of his work. The invasion of Iraq and the long chaos that followed were a turning point. Fukuyama broke with the neocon friends and mentors who had shaped his career until then. His first published piece of writing, in 1979, had been in Norman Podhoretz’s Commentary magazine, and he had once revered the Iraq War architect Paul Wolfowitz as “one of the smartest people I had ever met.” But after that war was launched, in 2003, he stopped speaking with them. He even found himself taking a particularly awkward silent elevator ride with the conservative columnist Charles Krauthammer. By the late 2000s, Fukuyama was referring to himself as a “realistic Wilsonian.” He is still idealistic about democracy, but he sees that democracy alone is not some kind of fairy dust that can be sprinkled on randomly chosen countries; it is meaningless without institutions and a strong state to ensure the rule of law. His work from over the past two decades has supported this point—especially his two-book Political Order series. “Creating a modern, effective state was a much more difficult task than creating a democracy,” he writes in the memoir, looking back at America’s dead ends in Iraq and Afghanistan. Among the unsexy chapters are those in which he extols the value of an impersonal bureaucracy as crucial for a well-functioning polity, as opposed to one in which a leader hands out jobs to cronies and personally enriches himself. Liberal democracy will not be dethroned, Fukuyama insists—mostly because no one has come up with a better idea. In response to the global rise of anti-liberal movements, many people across the political spectrum have blamed liberalism itself, casting it as a dilapidated system that should be torn down to the studs. But as far as Fukuyama is concerned, every alternative vision has either already been discredited or would lead to a worse world. The ultimate value of liberalism, he argues, is that it delivers on a fundamental human need better than any other framework (though it could still use plenty of tweaking). We are defined, he explains, by thymos, a drive for recognition. Almost as much as we need food to live, we demand to have our worth acknowledged. We want respect. And this can be fully realized only in a liberal society where all people have equality under the law. Although “stuff”—Fukuyama’s word—might still happen, since the late 18th century, an increasing number of people have lived with a political system that has at least the potential to scratch everyone’s thymos. And this is why we have reached the end. Full stop. But there is a problem: It’s really dull here. As much as we desire the autonomy and dignity that come with recognition, we are also drawn to the risk and struggle that were involved in achieving that recognition in the first place. Just because liberal democracies now satisfy the thymotic urge, this doesn’t mean that humans don’t still want something to die for. As Fukuyama explains it in the memoir, “the peace and prosperity brought about by a liberal order based on equal recognition is not enough for many people. They do not want to be recognized as the equals of every other person on earth.” Our horizons have been “flattened,” and it has made us restless and angry. Read: Trust makes a difference against the coronavirus This is sad. It is depressing to think that we are incapable of just enjoying the fruits of liberal democracy, and of doing the incremental renovation work of making it function even better for the largest number of people. Instead we “struggle for the sake of struggle,” as Fukuyama put it in his original essay, because we “cannot imagine living in a world without struggle.” Eventually, this means turning on liberal democracy itself—inventing conspiracy theories to stoke our fury, valorizing strongmen, idealizing deadly premodern practices and values—which is how Fukuyama understands the populist and antidemocratic rebellions of the past decade. All of this might sound cynical if it didn’t have some serious explanatory value. Take his critique of pro-Palestinian protests on college campuses. Without ignoring the very real death and destruction in Gaza, Fukuyama says these students are basically bored: “What they want is a break from their lives of steady but unheroic striving to get into those universities, and to experience danger and risk in pursuit of a just cause that has never previously been a major part of their lives. No more than the MAGA right do they have a clear vision of what type of future society they want to live in.” We have plenty of energy for tearing things down, he thinks, but liberal democracy is a logic problem that has already been solved, and it cannot be unsolved or reconfigured. Fukuyama wants to be able to say I told you so. And on these grounds, his book does remind us that he did. But there is little comfort in this. What he has identified is a human problem: the blink of an eye that separates utopia from dystopia. We got what we always wanted, but it is not enough and never will be. “We have means of distributing resources a bit more evenly and should undertake to do that,” Fukuyama, the ever lucid analyst, writes. “What we lack is a way of distributing respect.” That desire for recognition won’t be sated, and this fact is the most dangerous thing for all of us. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

This Was a Good Sculpture
2026-09-09

This Was a Good Sculpture

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Until last week, a big electric-blue sculpture by Joel Shapiro sat next to the Kennedy Center. Boxy and rigid but seemingly weightless, Blue looked like a clumsy person who’d been swept by a breeze into an accidental pirouette. Shapiro called the metalwork a “celebration of possibility.” Some people thought it looked like Gumby. I loved it. The leaders of the Kennedy Center—and there is really just one leader of the Kennedy Center—did not. Workers dismantled Blue last Wednesday. No specific reason was given, save that the change had occurred at the center’s request. Internal documents obtained by The New York Times say that the sculpture was targeted for removal soon after Donald Trump took control of the institution. Shapiro—one of the most important American sculptors of his generation—died last summer. He spent decades coaxing human gesture and emotion from the austere geometry of minimalism. His abstract shapes and forms have strong personalities. They lie in wait for their audience, then suddenly come alive. I knew Shapiro a little, through his wife, the artist Ellen Phelan. He was owlish, warm, blunt, and inspiring to be around, always searching for what he called a “little moment of rapture.” Blue was one of those. It wasn’t gilded. Shapiro spent two years in India in the Peace Corps, which President John F. Kennedy established. The antecedent for the Kennedy Center was right there in New Delhi: the architect Edward Durell Stone’s striking new American embassy, which he would channel in his later design for a national arts center—which would be named for Kennedy after his assassination. Decades later, Shapiro made monumental works for American diplomatic missions overseas. Blue, fittingly, stood near the Kennedy Center’s Peace Corps Gallery. Washington’s landscape is full of these cords of connection—some accidental, some carefully arranged. This one was simply cut, leaving a blemish in a city full of symbols. Those symbols are everywhere. You can walk from the Kennedy Center to the United States Holocaust Memorial Museum in a brisk half hour. I’ve taken this route frequently, usually while running. The parks, the monuments, the cherry trees, and the limestone edifices of federal power all prompt questions: about who gets memorialized, what myths and whose money might explain why, and who’s missing. One reason I repeat this route so often is to keep an inventory. What moved? What vanished? What’s next? Some landmarks—the State Department, in Foggy Bottom, for instance—haven’t changed but were hollowed by the Trump administration’s personnel cuts. The Lincoln Memorial is no longer visible in the botched Reflection Pool, an eyesore that’s been drained again to fix a shoddy renovation. Read: The Kennedy Center has a problem that courts can’t fix Keep going, past the memorials to World War II and the Korean War, and, nearby, Maya Lin’s black-granite scar, elegizing U.S. troops in Vietnam. I love odd and nerdy things along this route: statues of 19th-century Latin American liberators, Cold War allegorical bronzes, and, tucked away, Robert Berks’s playful Einstein. (People like to sit on his lap and rub his nose.) Einstein said, “Force always attracts men of low morality.” Another cord of connection: Berks made the giant bronze bust of Kennedy that resides inside the Kennedy Center. Push farther, past the Jefferson Memorial and the Tidal Basin, until Independence Avenue and the Holocaust Museum—which had its own upheaval last year, when Trump purged its board of trustees—come into view. Here, less than two miles from the Kennedy Center, stands another big Joel Shapiro—his portentously titled Loss and Regeneration. It’s one of his most painful, deeply moving works. Two black-bronze forms: a windowless house crashed upside down on its roof and, 100 feet away, an unstable but determined tree-like shape. Life after genocide. The house is inscribed with lines translated from a poem by an unnamed child in the Terezín ghetto: I’d be well again. Then I’d like to live And go back home again. Shapiro made this work in 1993. Blue arrived at the Kennedy Center in 2019, a gift from the artist and a centerpiece of the REACH, the campus’s elegant expansion that brought more art and more audiences. Blue and Loss became the cardinal points of my run: possibility and devastation. I doubt anyone else did this, but I got in the habit of tap-touching them: Blue at the start. Loss before I headed home. President Dwight Eisenhower authorized the creation of a national cultural center in Washington, D.C. But Kennedy worked hard to bring it into existence. Almost two years into his presidency, he gave a nationally televised speech to launch a public campaign for it—with appearances by Marian Anderson, Leonard Bernstein, Robert Frost, Harry Belafonte, and a 7-year-old Yo-Yo Ma. Kennedy spoke that night about art being political “in the most profound sense, not as a weapon in the struggle but as an instrument of understanding” among humanity. That was a perfect fit for Shapiro. I love how he could weld whimsy and catastrophe: a big blue dancer a short walk from an overturned house and a dark tree. But he also said that his sculptures have an “obdurate” sense of self. Blue will carry that obdurate spirit with it—down the National Mall to the National Gallery of Art. David Rubenstein, the chairman of the museum’s board and the former chairman of the Kennedy Center (he was ousted by Trump), just bought it from Shapiro’s foundation. That walk from whimsy to catastrophe just got shorter.
