The Anniversary I Wish I Didn’t Share With My Husband
2026-09-09

The Anniversary I Wish I Didn’t Share With My Husband

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
During a visit in May to the Maritime Museum in Halifax, Nova Scotia, I watched a film about a 1917 cargo-ship explosion that killed more than 1,700 townspeople and injured many more. The grainy black-and-white images of the flattened city catapulted me back to the devastation of Lower Manhattan after terrorists attacked the World Trade Center on September 11, 2001. Halifax suffered a blizzard following the cargo-ship catastrophe, and the sight of the city covered in a deep blanket of snow recalled the dust and debris from the once-glimmering towers that coated the Financial District. When I heard the recorded voices of the Haligonian survivors recounting their memories, I walked out of the small theater to cry. Twenty-five years on from September 11, 2001, an image or memory can still evoke the very moment I learned that my 36-year-old redheaded husband, Jeffrey R. Smith, would not be coming home from work that day. I, too, am now a voice in a museum. At the National September 11 Memorial & Museum, any visitor can replay my family’s intensely personal memories of a national horror. Years before the museum had a building, curators teamed up with StoryCorps, an oral-history nonprofit, and tried to make it easy for families to contribute by giving us a special number to call and record from home. Making this call felt like a leap of faith. How would these recordings be used? Where would they be stored? Still, my two daughters—who were then 8 and 10 years old—and I decided we wanted to participate. Ellen S. Bakalian: Everything my husband wasn’t there for I remember sitting on the couch while the girls took turns, cupping their hands over my cellphone in a semblance of privacy. I also recall one of them wandering the house, chatting like a teenager. I remember wanting them to practice what they would say, and I wanted to hear it before they called, but my girls had their own ideas. I found it nerve-racking until I realized that whatever they said would be straight from the hearts of girls who were not yet 3 and not yet 1 when their father was killed. I haven't heard the recordings. I recently thought that I wanted to, but I couldn't imagine donning headphones and listening to my voice or my daughters’ amid strangers at the museum. I contacted the chief curator, who kindly offered a private place for me to revisit what we had said. I made a date, then canceled it. I doubt I will reschedule. I know that if I listened, the editor in me would wish we could rerecord, and the rest of me would fall apart. When we were in Nova Scotia, my friends and I took turns reading historical plaques out loud. I always make a point of reading the names on memorials wherever I find them. Years ago, one Presidents’ Day weekend, my daughters and I took the train to Washington, D.C. I remember standing in front of the Vietnam Veterans Memorial and deciding that we would read one young soldier’s name aloud as we traced our fingers over the letters. We looked up a 19-year-old soldier on a sheaf of laminated paper that was hanging on the wall and read the name of his hometown. We said a prayer for him and his family because, as I remember explaining to the girls, “one day Daddy’s name will be on a plaque somewhere, and we want people to read his name out loud.” The name Jeffrey R. Smith can be found by museumgoers in Lower Manhattan, and is also on two plaques at the University of Rochester, where Jeff earned both his undergraduate and master’s degrees. I went with my daughters to see these tributes when the girls were 18 and 16. We walked in Jeff’s footsteps. We read all of the names out loud. Unlike the men and women whose names appear on the Vietnam memorial, Jeff and the thousands of other innocent victims that Tuesday morning were not casualties of war. This is an important distinction, a line that I, like most September 11 widows and widowers, believe should not be blurred. America was senselessly and successfully attacked by terrorists, and Jeff, along with nearly 3,000 other civilians, was killed while going about an otherwise ordinary day. From the September 2026 issue: The man who refused to sit on the sidelines Knowing that our voices are in a museum because our profound personal loss is part of this country’s history is both gratifying and unsettling. I am glad that there is such a powerful monument to that awful day. I am grateful that my husband and the other innocent victims are commemorated each year with a moment of silence. But I am staggered to be part of something this big, forever and ever. On this anniversary, I think of the couples who get to experience such milestones differently—joyfully, gratefully, together. Jeff and I will never have that. This is the pain that lurks just below the surface, only to bubble up without warning, like in a museum in Halifax. I know, of course, that I am one story, one tragedy, among many. But within our national catastrophe, which changed so much about our collective understanding of security, of terror, is my own private and persistent grief.
The Atlantic
Mothers for Sale
2026-09-09

Mothers for Sale

The Atlantic | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
This article was featured in the One Story to Read Today newsletter. Sign up for it here. When Shoshannah Roberts talks about the four babies she’s given away, she glows. “When I think about it now, it still feels emotional,” she told me. “Those moments are so magical.” For many women, the thought of handing over a newborn to someone else is almost incomprehensible—they feel a deep revulsion at a primal sundering. But for Roberts, acting as a surrogate four times is the most meaningful thing she has done with her life. “I’ve seen the parents hold their babies right away, skin to skin. I mean, like, raw,” she said. “Baby’s off me, cord is cut, and Mom is just holding her baby and sobbing. It’s amazing.” Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. The first time I saw Roberts, who lives in Santa Barbara County, California, she was speaking at the Men Having Babies conference in downtown San Francisco. Men Having Babies does exactly what its name promises: The organization helps gay aspiring fathers find women who are willing—eager, even—to carry their children. For one weekend every year, more than 200 men from across California and beyond fill the ballroom at the Marines’ Memorial Club & Hotel and listen to panel discussions and keynotes, with a special breakout session for single dads. Since the legalization of same-sex marriage, male couples have become the big growth market for American surrogacy, but the practice is booming among aspiring parents—married or single, straight or gay—who can afford the cost of hiring someone else to carry their child. In 2024, 11,821 embryos were transferred to surrogates in American clinics associated with the Society for Assisted Reproductive Technology, up from 8,461 three years earlier, and the United Nations estimates that the global worth of the industry is almost $15 billion. Most surrogacy in America today is commercial, meaning that the carrier is paid, and gestational, meaning that the carrier has no genetic relationship to the child. Barely a week passes without another celebrity posing with their new baby on Instagram—perhaps in scrubs or doing skin-to-skin contact—and thanking their heroic surrogate. Kim Kardashian, Anderson Cooper, Meghan Trainor, and Chrissy Teigen have all used surrogates. Some celebrities, citing medical risks, hire a surrogate to take on the dangers of pregnancy for them. Others want more children than they can have biologically. Hilaria and Alec Baldwin had a sixth baby via surrogate five months after Hilaria gave birth to their fifth child. (She has since carried their seventh.) But just as paid surrogacy is becoming a more conspicuous part of American life, so, too, is the backlash. When the actor Lily Collins, then 35, announced the birth of her baby, Tove, and her “endless gratitude for our incredible surrogate,” she and her husband were bombarded with messages describing them as “human traffickers” and “baby buyers.” In the past year, multiple horror stories—fraudulent agencies, human trafficking, elderly parents who are unable to take care of a newborn—have emerged, complicating what is usually presented as an uplifting narrative. If your primary concern is reproductive freedom, most surrogacy stories will seem inspirational. But if you factor in the risks to carriers, or simply feel skeptical of unregulated commerce, your doubts will multiply. In my experience, many liberal Americans are surprised to discover that their country is an outlier in its enthusiasm for commercial surrogacy. In Britain, where I live, a ban on the practice enjoys substantial support among feminists, who worry about the power differential between paid surrogates and wealthy would-be parents. (Altruistic surrogacy, in which the surrogate carries a baby solely out of generosity, is less controversial and is permitted.) A recent UN report went even further, declaring that commercial surrogacy “constitutes the sale of children, which is a crime,” and that “surrogacy arrangements can amount to or resemble slavery.” In one recent scandal, Thai women who answered an advertisement for work as surrogates in the nation of Georgia had their passports confiscated and were coerced into donating their eggs. Several developing countries that have become hubs for surrogacy tourism have enacted laws to protect women from exploitation by wealthy foreigners. Thailand banned commercial surrogacy in 2015 after the “Baby Gammy” case, in which a surrogate was left caring for a disabled child. She alleged that she had become pregnant with twins and refused a request by the intended parents—one of whom was a convicted sex offender—to abort the twin with Down syndrome. Because so much can go wrong, every country in the European Union has banned commercial surrogacy. France’s centrist liberal president, Emmanuel Macron, has described the practice as “not compatible with the dignity of women.” Italy’s right-wing populist leader, Giorgia Meloni, has enacted one of the strictest anti-surrogacy frameworks in the world. Not only is domestic surrogacy banned, but any Italian citizen who brings home a baby born to an overseas surrogate risks jail and a fine of up to 1 million euros. Many other developed countries have either tightly restricted surrogacy or chosen, as Britain and Canada have, to allow only the altruistic version. Israel allows surrogacy but imposes a raft of conditions: The commissioning parents must not have more than two children already, must not have serious criminal convictions, and must be of the same religion as the surrogate. In general, Europeans think of Americans’ enthusiasm for paid surrogacy the way we regard their fondness for guns and capital punishment: inexplicable. The piquant difference, however, is that surrogacy is a cause of the American left rather than the right. Red states tend to have tighter limits on surrogacy. Louisiana, for example, restricts surrogacy to married heterosexual couples using their own sperm and eggs. By contrast, when New York legalized commercial surrogacy in 2020, LGBTQ activists hailed it as a triumph for “fertility equality.” The debate cuts across political traditions and defies easy categorization. When surrogacy goes well, the result is a happy family that would not otherwise have existed. When it goes wrong, however, it can do so in unpredictable and emotionally crushing ways. At its core, the ethical question is this: How much physical and emotional risk should individuals be allowed to take on in the name of a commercial transaction? California, the home of Hollywood and Silicon Valley, is the undisputed center of American surrogacy, and the practice there is infused with a distinct blend of crunchy vibes, social liberalism, and straightforward capitalism. Surrogacy costs in the state begin at about $150,000 for a single baby, according to promotional materials for a variety of agencies doing business there. The percentage paid to the surrogate varies widely. Many, many enterprises want to help make gay couples’ dreams of parenthood come true, and the Men Having Babies conference was underwritten by a number of them: IVF clinics, genetic-testing companies, and surrogacy agencies. If the sea of Zegna sneakers and stylish down jackets didn’t tell you that these guys have money, the brochures offering future fathers “white-glove service” and VIP packages made it clear. In the exhibitors’ hall, I passed an abundance of merch designed to reel clients in—candles bearing the instruction LIGHT ME WHEN MATCHED, onesies with I LOVE MY DADS on them, canvas bags with the legend DADDY VIBES, and my own favorite: multiple iterations of sperm-shaped stress balls. Some were dyed rainbow colors. This was aspirational parenthood, right here. Pictures of happy dads and adorable kids abounded, including on a banner featuring two ripped, waxed, shirtless guys draped in a Pride flag and cradling a newborn. Words such as bespoke, boutique, and concierge appeared frequently in the literature. Words such as episiotomy and mastitis did not. It was here that I learned the industry’s preferred term for a surrogate pregnancy: a journey. At its best, surrogacy showers happiness all around. Intended parents—or IPs, in industry jargon—receive a longed-for baby; their surrogate is well compensated and well treated, and has the satisfaction of knowing that she has helped create a family. A few days after the conference, Roberts told me over Zoom that she had been interested in surrogacy ever since Phoebe on Friends carried triplets for her brother, Frank. Her first experience was blissful: She carried for a single mom who met a partner midway through the pregnancy, and Roberts later attended their beach wedding, in Florida. When she went back to work at a corporate job, “it felt a little hollow,” she said. She joined the surrogacy agency Hatch as a coordinator. In addition to four surrogate pregnancies, she’s had four children of her own. “My grandmother had 12,” she said. “So I think I’m built for it.” In mid-June, she announced her ninth pregnancy—another baby of her own. In the United States, the only serious opposition to surrogacy comes from an unlikely coalition of radical feminists and socially conservative Catholics. The former see surrogacy as exploitation: In 1983’s Right-Wing Women, the feminist author Andrea Dworkin described surrogacy as part of the “brothel model” of society, where “women can sell reproductive capacities the same way old-time prostitutes sold sexual ones.” Catholics, meanwhile, have doctrinal objections to any artificial interference in pregnancy. The more typical American view, though, tends toward individualism, in line with the “My body, my choice” slogan popularized by the abortion-rights movement, and an instinctive suspicion of government regulation: If the surrogate is okay with it, what business is it of anyone else? Although you can’t legally sell a kidney—even America has its limits—consent is treated as a universal solvent for many other tricky ethical issues. Well-heeled intended parents can also dodge practical constraints faced by biological or adoptive parents. Many agencies place no hard restrictions on the number of children any single person or couple can commission. Some parents want to get to a “double-digit number,” Men Having Babies’ founder, Ron Poole-Dayan, said at the conference. In 2023, a court in Los Angeles discovered that the Chinese video-game billionaire Xu Bo was simultaneously seeking parental rights for four babies born via surrogacy and was in the process of fathering at least eight more. The children (all boys, at his request) were being raised by nannies in nearby Irvine until they could be taken to him in China, The Wall Street Journal reported. There are also no legally enforceable upper age limits on intended parents. In August 2025, two 6-year-old children were found in a caged-off section of a San Francisco apartment. Their father explained that he had commissioned twins as a single dad at the age of 67, and when one of the babies was diagnosed with severe autism, he found himself struggling to cope. The locked gate stopped the neurodivergent twin from injuring himself during tantrums or running out of the building into the road. “I’m a senior, first-time parent, single parent, to twins, without domestic help,” the 74-year-old told The San Francisco Standard after he was reported to child services. (To which I might respond: And you didn’t think about this earlier? ) Difficulties like these are why many adoption agencies suggest an upper age limit of about 50. The surrogacy industry does not have a regulator that enforces a “fit and proper person” test of the kind given to people who want to adopt a child. Last year, Pennsylvania lawmakers proposed closing a loophole in the state’s law that had allowed a sex offender—who was legally barred from adopting or fostering—to commission a baby. There are no restrictions on Americans commissioning a baby abroad, even if the other country’s health services or human-rights laws are far worse than those in the United States. America also stands virtually alone as a rich country that permits incoming surrogacy tourism from elsewhere. Many of these international intended parents are from China, where surrogacy is banned and where, until recently, family size was limited by the one-child policy. These customers have exacting standards. “They want a Caucasian surrogate to carry the baby, with preferably blond hair, blue eyes,” Dee Gulati of Surrogacy4All, in New York, told me. “And they want an egg donor who looks like a cheerleader.” The resulting babies are automatically American citizens. (Hoping to change this, President Trump in August signed an executive order denying citizenship to children born to foreign citizens through commercial surrogacy.) In the U.S., no federal laws regulate surrogacy, and contracts related to the practice are unenforceable in many states. The Society for Ethics in Egg Donation and Surrogacy provides a “self-regulation framework” for agencies, but its greatest sanction is removing membership. The lack of regulation means that success depends more on luck and personal benevolence than on industry standards or legal guardrails. The primary question asked of parents attempting this new American way of birth is: Can you afford it? For some surrogates, handing over the baby is far harder than it was for Shoshannah Roberts. Last fall, in Chattanooga, Tennessee, I met Erika Jones, a quiet, self-contained woman who wore a discreet gold cross around her neck. She told me that her three ambitions in life had always been to visit Ireland, to be a surrogate, and to see a koala in person. She had wanted to be a surrogate since she was very young, before she’d even had her own two children. A few years ago, when she found herself settled in Fairfield, Ohio, with a good job and a stable income, she registered with an agency. The story of one couple particularly moved her. The wife, whom I’ll call Priya, had lost her brother to cancer in 2020. He’d banked his sperm before he died, and his family hoped to use it to fertilize a donor egg. The resulting embryo would then be carried by a surrogate and raised by Priya and her husband, allowing a part of her brother to live on. After Jones, then 34, learned the baby’s sex, she spent $40 on a balloon filled with pink confetti and had it sent to Priya, who recorded her reaction when she opened the box. But Jones didn’t see what she had hoped to see. “You could tell that she was disappointed that it was a girl,” Jones said. “I think she wanted a boy, to closely remind her of her brother.” Would the baby inside her be treated with respect, Jones wondered—or as second-best? From that point, in Jones’s telling, the relationship between the two women began to sour. Jones found Priya fussy and controlling, and felt that she was being blamed when a scan found that the fetus was growing unusually slowly. “I was like, I’m not a slave,” she said. She even wondered briefly about trying to keep the baby, but decided that wouldn’t be right. When Jones found mold and detected carbon monoxide in her apartment, in Ohio, she decided to move back home to Tennessee, closer to her family. That would violate her 57-page “gestational carrier agreement,” in which she had agreed to “take all necessary and reasonable steps” to give birth at UC Health in West Chester, Ohio. The intended parents told her that if the baby was born in Tennessee, they would face “significant legal challenges.” Because of the state’s more restrictive laws, they feared, the contract might not be valid there. Priya and her husband suggested that Jones stay put and send her children to Tennessee to stay with a friend or relative for the remaining five months of the pregnancy. Jones declined. She moved back to Tennessee but agreed to keep traveling to Ohio for medical appointments and then give birth there. She was being paid $45,000 plus expenses for the pregnancy but found that the frequent back-and-forth between states, in addition to her time off work while sick, had eaten into that payment. She cut off direct contact with Priya and emailed the agency asking that she not attend the birth. “I know I signed the contract—I am well aware and I intend on honoring it,” Jones wrote, “but it does not need to continue to be thrown in my face every other day.” She also bought a puppy, aware that she would soon be coming home from the hospital empty-handed; she wanted, she later wrote in a memoir, something “alive, soft and ours.” When Jones went into labor, she brought her two children, then ages 10 and 12, to the hospital, along with Susan Harris, a trusted friend, who alerted Priya and her husband. The labor was long—37 hours, 22 of them without an epidural. When Jones told the midwives that she wanted to push, the room suddenly filled with people. Earlier in her labor, Jones had asked for five minutes with the baby before handing her over. But the moment the little girl was delivered, she said, someone shouted, “No contact!” All she saw was a glimpse of dark-brown hair as the baby was taken away. She was not even told her birth weight. (Harris confirmed this account.) Priya told The Atlantic that she wasn’t “in any way disappointed” that the baby was a girl. She felt that because Jones had signed a legal contract agreeing to not have any skin-to-skin contact with the baby, she didn’t have to honor Jones’s request to hold her. “Maybe if you were nicer,” she thought. She has since ignored Jones’s request to talk through what happened. “At the end of the day, this is a business. We’re not friends,” she told The Atlantic. “You’re not doing me a favor. You’re getting paid $5,000 a month.” Both parties had fulfilled their side of the contract, she said, and it was time to move on. Jones has not found that so easy. She told me that the experience kept playing in her mind: “I felt like I was treated less than a human, and less than an animal.” According to the industry, unhappy surrogates are extremely rare. The owner of the surrogacy agency that Priya used told me that Jones’s experience was an “outlier,” one of only three cases out of the hundreds she had overseen that had “gone south.” At the Men Having Babies conference, an agency director assured the attendees that almost all of the “horror stories” reported in the press involved traditional surrogacy, in which the woman carrying the baby uses her own egg. (She added that the traditional approach now accounts for only 5 percent of American surrogacies.) The sales pitch to intended parents for gestational surrogacy—involving an egg from either the commissioning mother or a separate donor—is precisely that the surrogate has no legal claim to custody. That makes it legally safer for the rich couples who pay the bills than a traditional surrogacy. But it may be more physically dangerous for the surrogate. At the Men Having Babies conference, the first audience question was a stark one: How many surrogates die giving birth? Deaths are very rare, Poole-Dayan, the organizer, assured them. A 2020 study found that gestational surrogates were no more likely to die in childbirth than any other pregnant woman. Yet early evidence suggests that carrying a child who is not genetically related to you could increase the risk of complications: A 2024 study of more than 850,000 pregnancies over a decade in Ontario found that “severe maternal morbidity and adverse pregnancy outcomes” were three times higher among gestational carriers than in conventional pregnancies. When the mother and the fetus are not genetically related, the developmental biologist Emma Hilton has written, “Mum’s immune system may ramp up to fight against the wholly foreign tissue. In response, placental invasion becomes more aggressive.” (A systematic review from 2015 found that “most studies reporting on surrogacy have serious methodological limitations.”) Because of the elevated risks associated with twin pregnancies, most reputable agencies now implant only one embryo at a time, but—a recurring theme in this story—no law or professional regulation requires them to do so. To Poole-Dayan, the value of a contract and of compensation is that it forces both parties to have uncomfortable conversations while the risks are still theoretical. “I always tell people, ‘Imagine yourself at the funeral of the surrogate,’ ” he told me. I found Poole-Dayan’s forthrightness a refreshing change from the cloying online culture around surrogacy, with the staged hospital shots and cutesy euphemisms designed to play down the toll of pregnancy and birth. In one video, a little girl born via surrogacy describes the woman who carried her—who built her, cell by cell, inside her own body—as an “Easy-Bake Oven.” When Jones concluded that she had been mistreated, she turned to Kallie Fell. A perinatal nurse by training, Fell works out of an office the size of a janitor’s closet in a co-working space in Pleasant Hill, east of San Francisco. Fell is the executive director of the Center for Bioethics and Culture Network, which was founded in 2000 to campaign against “Big Fertility” but in recent years has also opposed youth gender transition. The CBC has only two full-time employees; Fell told me that it is “lonely work.” Her inbox is full of questions, unhappy stories, and requests for legal help: a surrogate left with medical debt after the intended parents ghosted her, a man who regrets being a sperm donor, women who don’t want to abort a fetus with disabilities but have signed a contract promising to do so. “I’m a little bit of a magnet,” she said. One of the most frequent problems, Fell told me, is the financial imbalance between a surrogate and the intended parents. Ethical agencies will ask the IPs to pay for an independent lawyer to read the contract on their surrogate’s behalf, but no law or regulation enforces this. Contracts therefore tend to be written with the protection of the IPs in mind rather than the carrier. Surrogates can be asked to sign nondisclosure agreements, to protect the privacy of the IPs and the child, but these can also prevent them from speaking honestly about bad experiences. Mental-health troubles can be grounds for disqualification for potential surrogates, but IPs typically face less scrutiny. Last year, Wired reported on a woman with bipolar disorder who blamed her surrogate for having a stillbirth; in a fury, she sent a photo of the corpse to an iPad belonging to the surrogate’s 7-year-old son. The same imbalance is true of criminal records: Another woman who’d contacted Fell, Amanda Misbahu, told me that midway through her surrogate pregnancy—arranged via Facebook—she began to worry that the IP, a gay single father, hadn’t been entirely honest about his criminal history. To me, Misbahu’s story sounded like a surrogacy nightmare, but she cheerfully insisted that she would be willing to act as a surrogate again. Misbahu’s upbringing had been turbulent. She had been taken away from her own mother when she was six months old, she said, with “maggots in my diapers and Pepsi in my bottles,” and had been raised by a traditionally minded, religious family. The turmoil continued into her adulthood, in successive marriages that didn’t work out. By age 23, Misbahu had given birth to five babies, but none of them lived with her full-time; some were being raised primarily by relatives. In her 30s, her thoughts turned to surrogacy. “It was a way for me, in a sentence, to help me through some of my own trauma,” she said. Giving up her own children had left her distraught, she said, yet “with this one, I knew the outcome, I knew it was going to happen, and it was a situation that I knew would be traumatic but I could control.” Two agencies turned her down: the first because of her weight, and the second after her early psychological trauma came up in a medical screening. So she went on Facebook to advertise her services directly to would-be parents. When a man whom I’ll call Cameron got in contact with Misbahu, she wrote her own surrogacy contract, and “he let me pick the egg donor—it was like online dating,” she said. She and Cameron wanted two embryos transferred, but the fertility doctor advised only one. “We said, We’ll take our $100,000 and we’ll go next door,” she told me. The doctor relented but made her sign a waiver. Only one of the embryos implanted. At eight weeks, she was hospitalized for life-threatening blood clots. She needed blood thinners for the rest of her pregnancy. “I thought she was gonna die,” her fourth child, Alyssa, told me. “So I would just start freaking out randomly when I thought about it—like, at school, sometimes I’d be crying in lunch.” Although Misbahu didn’t know it, Cameron had once pleaded no contest to involuntary manslaughter in California after injecting a friend with a fatal dose of Benadryl in his apartment. He then moved to Kentucky and practiced as a physician’s assistant without a license, a deception that came to light after patients claimed that he had botched their cosmetic surgeries. In 2001, he’d been sentenced to 20 years in prison for five counts of second-degree assault and 112 counts of wanton endangerment, although he was released early. In learning about his past, Misbahu also discovered that he had an adult daughter. In the end, she concluded that he’d turned his life around and that there was nothing she could do except go through with the contract. Cameron and his new boyfriend were present for the birth, for which Misbahu had an epidural for the first time in her history of deliveries, “because I’m like, I don’t want to have to feel any of this,” she told me. She cried—from the hormones, she recalled—and saw the baby a few hours later: “And I was like, She’s so pretty, but I really just can’t even touch her.” Misbahu attended counseling sessions for six months after the birth. “You felt like you lost a baby,” she said. “And I wouldn’t stop pumping milk.” Eventually, a friend told her that she had to move on, and so she donated the breast milk to a local bank for premature babies. Her counselor wasn’t much help, she said: “She thought it was the coolest thing; she had never met a surrogate. She just wanted me to paint pictures in her office. And I was like, Ma’am, I feel like I want to blow out my fucking brains.” Misbahu told me that she even gave her gun to her brother because she was so worried about her mental health. A few months after we first spoke, I met Misbahu in a pancake restaurant on the outskirts of Columbus, Ohio. She turned up bearing a knitted kangaroo that her mother had made for me, and she wore a medical-alert bracelet around her wrist. Her clotting condition had convinced her that she could not advertise herself as a surrogate again. However, she had married a Ghanaian asylum seeker whom she’d met on a dating app, and was trying for a baby with him to strengthen his claim. (They have since split.) For a time, Misbahu had remained in contact with Cameron—and had even helped him find his next surrogate. But eventually, their relationship broke down. She had asked him to buy a present for her daughter, and he’d told her that she couldn’t treat him as an ATM. “Finally, I wrote him back, and I was like, Look, I almost literally died giving birth to your kid. Like, it doesn’t mean you owe me anything. But that was pretty disrespectful,” she told me. (Cameron did not respond to questions from The Atlantic.) He blocked her on Facebook. But one of her friends could still see his page, so she used that account to secretly watch his daughter—the baby she had carried—grow up. She showed me a photo of the little girl and her siblings on her phone. “The girls are so pretty,” she said with pride. Surrogacy has a supply problem. There are not enough ethically motivated, healthy, middle-class American women to meet demand. Speakers at the Men Having Babies conference repeatedly stressed that reputable agencies accept only 5 percent of applicants as surrogates. Good agencies screen women for health and psychological problems, and question them about their finances and the supportiveness of their partner. Those safeguards are reflected in the high costs. Look abroad, though, and it’s a different story. Some agencies offer “guaranteed baby” packages for as little as $50,000. Surrogacy destinations pop up anywhere the local laws are lax, such as Northern Cyprus, a territory that exists in a legal limbo and is recognized only by Turkey. (The problem is that babies born there can end up stateless.) For parents from Russia, nearby Georgia is a similar no-questions-asked destination. India used to be the most favored low-cost destination, but in 2015, Prime Minister Narendra Modi, citing fears of exploitation, banned commercial surrogacy for foreigners. Ukraine’s appeal as a surrogacy destination has diminished since Russia’s invasion in 2022, although the leading agency there, BioTexCom, told me that the company is still handing off one baby born via surrogacy every day on average, at a cost of about $60,000. A representative of the agency initially told me that it was planning to open a clinic in Kazakhstan, to cater to the growing Asian market. In July, however, that country abruptly banned foreign surrogacy amid concerns about exploitation. BioTexCom takes a more unsentimental approach to surrogacy than American agencies. When I asked Denis Herman, the legal director, what his best day on the job had been, I expected him to tell me about uniting a baby with happy parents. Instead, he talked about business metrics: “I’m really focused on the KPIs, on getting the milestones done.” BioTexCom’s founder, Albert Tochilovsky, told me that he worried about falling birth rates, and presented surrogacy as an alternative to immigration. BioTexCom agreed to let me speak with one of its current surrogates, a 40-year-old whom it identified as Nana, and assigned an employee to translate our conversation. This was Nana’s third surrogate pregnancy since 2021, and she had two much older children of her own. During the first surrogate pregnancy, she was given an apartment to live in with her kids, she told me, so she’d decided to do it twice more; it was her only job. (Compensation for BioTexCom surrogates begins at $23,000, according to the company. The average yearly salary in Ukraine is about $8,700.) The image that Nana and the agency presented was of surrogacy as a sensible economic option and a morally good one. “In Ukraine, we say: Children are a flower of our life,” the translator told me. Mexico is more convenient for Americans, and a journey might cost $100,000 less there than in the States. But babies born in Mexico through surrogacy are given a Mexican passport by default, which can cause problems at the U.S. border. The State Department warns Americans that “there is no legal framework to protect parents who pursue surrogacy in Mexico, despite agencies and clinics promoting Mexico as a safe, legal destination for commercial surrogacy.” Wes Johnson-Ellis of the British firm My Surrogacy Journey has sufficient faith in the Mexican system that he and his husband, Michael, had their third child through a surrogate in Mexico City. Yet he acknowledges the concern that carriers in the country are taking on medical risk out of economic need. “Let’s not be naive,” Johnson-Ellis told me. “In Mexico, the compensation that we offer is three times the average wage, so it is a significant amount of money for them.” He estimated that his team accepts only 20 percent of the women who apply to be surrogates, and he said that they filter out anyone who is motivated only by money. Several surrogacy agencies told me that they worry deeply about the booming market in Mexico, and Johnson-Ellis expressed doubts about his company’s competitors there. “They don’t operate in an ethical way,” he said. “They don’t safeguard everyone; they don’t look after everyone.” Gulati, the practice director of Surrogacy4All, which arranges approximately 200 surrogate pregnancies every year, said that his favored destination for a low-cost journey is now Ghana, where he charges intended parents a flat rate of $49,000. Those carriers are under close watch. “The great thing about doing surrogacy in Ghana is that they live in a dormitory which is right near the hospital, so they can’t drink, they can’t smoke, they can’t do drugs,” he said. “During that period of time, they are monitored 24 hours, seven days a week. And as a result, the success rate with surrogacy is very, very high.” Even in California, the regulation of agencies leaves much to be desired. In May 2025, the caregivers of a two-month-old baby were reported to social services for potential abuse. When the police raided their mansion in Arcadia, a small city northeast of Los Angeles, they discovered another 15 children, who were being homeschooled by a platoon of nannies. “There were probably anywhere from six to eight at the time that we were there,” Captain Kollin Cieadlo of the Arcadia Police Department told me. Footage from surveillance cameras showed that the children were being drilled in English and Mandarin and forced, as a form of discipline, to do squats and other physical exercises that the police considered “above and beyond what a normal punishment would be.” Further searches revealed six more kids whose birth certificates declared them to also be the children of Silvia Zhang and Guojun Xuan, who owned the house. The couple had been running an agency, Mark Surrogacy, with only one client—themselves. In 2023, one of their surrogates had been obligated, thanks to the prebirth order she had signed, to hand over a newly delivered child to people she had never seen before, who arrived at the hospital with power-of-attorney documents. The sheer number of parental orders obtained by Zhang and Xuan during this period triggered an investigation ordered by a judge, but no further action was taken. As news of the mansion raid spread, surrogates then currently under contract with Mark Surrogacy—and still pregnant—began finding one another in Facebook groups. They all told similar stories, of being commissioned by a woman who said that she was unable to conceive and desperately wanted a child. When Zhang and Xuan were questioned by the police, they denied that anything was amiss. “They simply said they just wanted a large family with a lot of kids,” Cieadlo said. The children went into foster care, and one of the surrogates, Kayla Elliott of Corpus Christi, Texas, started a GoFundMe to try to win custody of the child she’d carried. (She has since said that she believes the baby should be left with the foster parents, for continuity of care.) No charges have been filed against Zhang and Xuan, who have since commissioned even more babies. Although they could face police action over the allegations of child abuse, the startling fact is that their baby-creation spree is legal in California. (Their lawyers did not respond to a request for comment.) The Wall Street Journal reported that the police were investigating the couple for possible trafficking, but Zhang told the newspaper that she intended to raise all of the children: “We never sell our babies. We take care of them very well.” After speaking with Jones and Misbahu, I spent hours turning over their stories in my mind, trying to reconcile their bad experiences with the many upbeat counterexamples I had seen at the Men Having Babies conference. None of those women had needed surrogacy to stay solvent, and more than one had joked to me that they could have earned more as an Uber driver. For Veronica Bowers, a two-time surrogate who now works for the Northwest Surrogacy Center in Oregon, surrogacy was a way for her to be with her own kids while still contributing to the household finances. (For similar reasons, military wives are actively recruited by surrogacy agencies.) “It is a badge of honor,” Bowers told me. “I feel really proud that I did this.” The happiest surrogacy experiences I saw resembled open adoptions, where the process is transparent and the birth mother can stay in touch with the child. (Bowers and Roberts are in contact with the children they carried—like an aunt rather than a mother.) The bad stories involved parents who behaved as though they were ordering a baby from a mail-order catalog. I was also left deeply uneasy by the realities of international surrogacy: Why should California be a place where Chinese parents go to find blond, blue-eyed surrogates for status reasons—and why should American parents have the right to acquire a cut-rate child by employing a Ghanaian woman to live in a dormitory under 24/7 monitoring? To protect everyone involved, states should impose upper limits on the age of commissioning parents and on the number of children any couple or individual can commission, as well as an equalization of the disclosure requirements. If surrogates must reveal their medical and criminal histories, then so should the rich people employing them. Something that surprised me was that many of the women I interviewed who had found surrogacy to be a traumatic experience were ambivalent, rather than flatly opposed, to the practice. Jones said that she hadn’t ruled out another journey, although she was still suffering medical complications from the difficult pregnancy and birth. She also worried that the baby girl she had carried would never know “three pertinent people in her life”—the biological father who had died before she was conceived, the egg donor who had provided half of her DNA, and the surrogate who had carried her for nine nausea-filled months. Jones had watched videos online of children born through surrogacy who opposed the practice, and she wondered whether she had been selfless to become a surrogate—or selfish. At the end of our meeting, she said—just as Amanda Misbahu did—that she had carried on pumping breast milk for months after the birth, even though she’d had no baby to nourish. She had also asked friends to check Priya’s social media to see what the baby she’d carried looked like now. On her phone, she showed me a photo of the little girl smiling alongside Priya’s other children. Other than that picture, Jones said, her hospital bracelet and blanket are her only mementos of carrying the girl. She still feels the loss: “Truthfully, even though she’s not my child, she’s my child.” This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “Mothers for Sale.” When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

But that's the big issue: no one can build a new reactor today. And no, not because of NRC regulations. America literally has no steel plants large enough to create a nuclear pressure vessel. These things are massive, and historically had to be transported by river or have specialized roads put in to move them. The last steel plant that could make these closed decades ago. "For very large 'Generation III+' reactors, production of the pressure vessel requires, or is best undertaken by, forging presses of about 140-150 MN (14-15,000 tonnes) capacity which accept hot steel ingots of 500-600 tonnes. These are not common, and individual large presses do not have high throughput – about four pressure vessels per year appears to be common at present, fitted in with other work, though the potential is greater than this. Reactor vendors prefer large forgings to be integral, as single products, but for large units split forgings are welded together. These welds then need checking through the life of the plant, and the fewer that are required in fabrication the better. Also, whereas Generation II reactors might have required some 2000 tonnes of forgings, the largest modern designs require about twice this amount. For example, Westinghouse has said that the minimum requirement for making the largest AP1000 components is a 15,000 tonne press taking 350 tonne ingots. The very heavy forging capacity in operation today is in Japan (Japan Steel Works), China (China First Heavy Industries, China Erzhong, Shanghai Electric), South Korea (Doosan, Taewoong), France (Le Creusot), and Russia (OMZ Izhora, Atommash, Petrozavodskmash, ZiO-Podolsk). New capacity is being built by these and other companies in Japan, China, South Korea, Czech Republic and Russia. New capacity is planned in India (Larsen & Toubro, Bharat Heavy Electricals, Bharat Forge Ltd). In China the Harbin Boiler Co. and SEC subsidiary SENPE are increasing capacity. Nothing in North America currently approaches these enterprises.* The changed position of the USA is remarkable. In the 1940s it manufactured over 2700 Liberty ships, each 10,800 tonne DWT – possibly pioneering modular construction at that scale (average construction time was 42 days in the shipyard). In the 1970s it had a substantial heavy infrastructure, but today China, Japan, South Korea, India, Europe and Russia are all well ahead of it. Steelmaker ArcelorMittal, based in Luxembourg, now owns the US company which built most US reactor pressure vessels in the 1970s-1980s. *In the 1970s, requirements were more modest: both US Steel and Bethlehem Steel had 8000 tonne presses and could handle 300 tonne ingots. US forging capacity has not been significantly upgraded since, partly due to lack of integration with steel mills and melt shops to supply the hot steel as 600-tonne ingots." https://world-nuclear.org/info... [world-nuclear.org]The smaller reactors people are talking about are totally non-viable. Nuclear works because of the scale. OPG has run the numbers and disagrees. On the bright side their SMR construction continues along on time so you will have some real examples to learn from soon. https://www.opg.com/projects-s... The NS equations are just a model of the physics of fluids. Particularly, they assume a continuum whereas actual fluids, having molecules and such, are not a continuum. For most engineering problems, you can assume the fluid is a continuum. At very small scales, such as at a singularity, such an assumption is not valid. There may be more comments in this discussion. Buddy...when the original Star Trek ran, it was a different world, especially in the case of what was allowed on television. The bedrooms of married couples showed twin beds. The first interracial kiss in US television history occurred on Star Trek, and that episode was banned across much of the South. Women were not considered worthy of positions of authority, especially if it included having authority over men. It was literally a different world. Until the advent of Star Trek, "science fiction" on television consisted of simple-minded fantasy series like Lost In Space, Time Tunnel, Land of the Giants, etc. Tropes such as faster-than-light travel and interstellar conflict, which Babylon 5 relied upon, barely existed before Star Trek. Even sub-plots that carried on for whole seasons were rare. Episodes were expected to stand alone. Let me put it this way: The original Star Trek swam in very different waters than Babylon 5, and it changed the rules in ways that allowed Babylon 5 to be what it was (which was excellent, by the way). News and Politics - Slate Magazine
We’re Pretty Sure We Know Who’s Going to Win the House
2026-09-09

We’re Pretty Sure We Know Who’s Going to Win the House

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. With the primaries mostly behind us, general-election season is here. We at the Slatest have been covering the midterms for a while, but getting wrapped up in individual races risks overlooking the more important question: Which party will control Congress once the dust has settled? It’s a big topic, so we’re taking it one chamber at a time. Later this week we’ll consider the Senate, but today we’re focusing on the House. OK, let’s start with the obvious: Which party is going to win? I don’t have a crystal ball, but history is instructive. In the modern era, the sitting president’s party almost always loses House seats in midterms. The opposite has happened only a few times: during the depths of the Great Depression, after Republicans overreached by moving to impeach Bill Clinton, and after the Sept. 11 attacks. On average, over the past 92 years of midterms, the party in power has lost 27 seats. Republicans are currently sitting on a single-digit majority. Doesn’t that mean that Democrats are a shoo-in? The odds are in their favor. Republicans are contending with high gas prices (thanks to Donald Trump’s war in Iran), voter discontent over the cost of living (thanks in part to Trump’s tariffs), and a vainglorious but deeply unpopular president. Democrats lead by about 6 points on the generic ballot, which measures which party voters prefer without taking individual candidates into consideration. Republicans are, dare we say, in a bit of disarray. As Politico reported last week, some GOP candidates representing harder-to-win districts don’t want the president to campaign for them this fall. Others, as my colleague Jim Newell explains, are anxious that Trump’s super PAC won’t spend money to help them except on ads that would yoke them to his unpopular record. There are a few reasons to think Democrats’ path to a majority may be rocky. Redistricting is one of them. Over the past couple of years, Republicans have broken with long-standing precedent and frenziedly redrawn their states’ congressional maps in a bid to advantage GOP candidates. (Democrats have responded in kind, though it’s worth remembering that the arms race kicked off in earnest when Trump demanded that Texas further gerrymander that state’s maps.) It hasn’t always worked; last week, Missouri’s Supreme Court blocked the state from using a more-GOP-friendly map in November. But as things stand, winning the House will probably be more of an uphill climb for Democrats than it otherwise would’ve been. Democrats are barreling toward Election Day with some liabilities of their own. Joe Biden’s presidency left a bad taste in most Americans’ mouths, and the party remains broadly unpopular. Many Democratic voters are unhappy with their party’s established leaders, and debates over support for Israel have riven the party’s base. In key Maine, Michigan, and California swing seats, Democrats have gambled on progressive nominees who may prove to be too far left for centrist electorates. So you’re saying Republicans have a chance? To be clear, it would be shocking if Democrats failed to win a House majority. But the margin also matters. A slim majority is harder to corral because a few lawmakers can hold the rest hostage to extract concessions. Unexpected resignations or deaths, a phenomenon both parties have dealt with this year, can become hugely consequential when only a few seats determine House control. Still, politics moves at light speed these days, and a lot could happen before most voters head to the polls. If Trump’s trade war or war in Iran winds down, it could boost GOP House candidates. But given both historical trends and present circumstances, Democrats are clear favorites to retake control. The Senate, as we’ll explore soon, is a much closer call. OK, that’s enough forecasting for one day. Let’s talk about something closer at hand—like how to spend the rest of your evening. My colleagues recommend: A suggestion for schools: As summer vacation comes to a close, students will likely transition to spending most of their time indoors, looking at screens. But what if they could have more unstructured time outdoors? Natalie Pattillo makes the case that recess is an easy way for students—especially teenagers—to unplug from their devices, reset their minds, and build their relationships. Some self-care advice: Finch, a popular wellness and habit-tracking app, promises to help users achieve their self-care goals with the assistance of a colorful cartoon bird. “It’s Tamagotchi for the burned out and overwhelmed,” writes Sara Jin Li. But it turns out that more screen time is not a great solution to feeling burned out and overwhelmed. Instead, there’s an easier, analog method that can do the trick. An old book to revisit: Octavia Butler fans, rejoice! After being out of print for decades, the author’s third novel, Survivor, has just been rereleased. Slate writer Rebecca Onion spoke with a scholar of Butler’s work about why science-fiction fans should read this book. A new book to explore: Marlon James’ new book, The Disappearers, follows a group of gay men grappling with extreme homophobia in 1980s Jamaica. It has already been called the “novel of the decade”—and it lives up to the hype, according to Slate critic Laura Miller. She writes, “The masterstroke of The Disappearers is that James lands the finish, a feat of generosity, compassion, and, as with the rest of this unforgettable book, unconquerable bravado.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Democrats Keep Looking for the “Next Obama.” They Should Do This Instead.
2026-09-09

Democrats Keep Looking for the “Next Obama.” They Should Do This Instead.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first time I remember hearing Barack Obama’s name was in late 2006 or early 2007. I was sitting in seventh grade social studies, where we were encouraged to discuss current events. “I think he’s going to run for president,” our teacher confided in a knowing yet slightly hushed tone, almost like she still couldn’t quite believe it. Back then, much of the excitement was bound up in the prospect of America having its first Black president, but Obama ultimately proved himself to be worthy of it. Two decades on, the energy he created has morphed into something decidedly more desperate. Obama is the only Democratic president this century to win reelection and remains one of the country’s most popular political figures. Which might explain why Democrats, looking ahead to congressional elections this fall and a 2028 presidential race whose stakes feel existential, keep looking for the former president’s spiritual successor: someone who can inspire, speak, and above all win, just like the real thing. All summer, voters, consultants, and pundits have repeatedly attached Obama’s name like a Homeric epithet to a bumper crop of young, talented, and mostly male candidates running across the country. It’s happened to Abdul El-Sayed, the party’s nominee for U.S. Senate in Michigan, whose self-confident swagger and immigrant-family story have invited the comparison. “It reminded me a lot of the Barack Obama bio,” Jon Favreau, Obama’s former speechwriter, recalled after seeing El-Sayed at a fundraiser. Then there’s James Talarico, the Texas state representative running for U.S. Senate whose winning primary campaign former New York Times columnist Charles Blow likened to Obama’s 2008 faceoff against Hillary Clinton. After Talarico’s campaign posted footage of the former president stumping with him at a taco shop this spring, the equivalencies flowed in. “I think you have what it takes to be the next Obama and Lord knows we need it!” one Facebook commenter wrote. The Obama parallel has similarly been lavished on two Democratic governors who look set to cruise to reelection this fall: Josh Shapiro of Pennsylvania, and Wes Moore of Maryland, who started fielding Obama analogies even before he became the state’s first Black governor. Pete Buttigieg, the former Biden transportation secretary who isn’t officially running for anything right now, has been getting the comparison for years. Even Graham Platner, the scruffy Maine oyster farmer, was winning Obama-esque praise before his Senate candidacy imploded. Jim Messina, Obama’s 2012 campaign manager, dubbed Platner’s victory speech on primary night a “masterclass” that recalled the 44th president. (A few weeks later, after an ex-girlfriend accused Platner of rape, Messina suggested Platner should “go jump in a Maine lake.”) Further south, Sen. Jon Ossoff, a Georgia Democrat who is up for reelection this fall, has been likened to the former president for viral speeches that are already earning him national attention and fueling 2028 chatter. The search for an Obama inheritor isn’t totally new. Ahead of the 2020 election, Joe Biden, Kamala Harris, and Cory Booker tussled over the former president’s legacy in what the Los Angeles Times perceptively dubbed “the Obama primary.” The search has taken on new proportions and greater urgency as Democrats once again seek to claw their way back to power after yet another Trump presidency. The question is: Even if they find their guy, is an Obama 2.0 what the party needs, now, or in 2028? To give Democrats their due, trying to re-create a popular two-term commander in chief in the aggregate doesn’t seem like the worst strategy in the world. After the Biden interregnum and Donald Trump’s return, nostalgia for better political times appears broadly felt: A January poll of a hypothetical 2028 third-term matchup against Trump found Obama ahead by double digits. (Neither man can legally be elected again.) Historical patterns are worth taking seriously, too. Every couple of decades, it seems, Democrats turn to a sunny, novel-seeming, relatively young man to resuscitate their party’s political fortunes. It happened with John F. Kennedy in 1960, Jimmy Carter in 1976, and Bill Clinton in 1992. And as Obama proved, lesser-known lawmakers with funny names can sometimes go on to make history. But there are a few reasons to wonder whether Democrats’ search for “the next Obama” has gotten a little out of hand—and might inadvertently lead the party even deeper into the political wilderness. For one thing, the comparison has become so common that it’s started to lose meaning, and power. The last few years of American politics are littered with Democrats hailed as the next Obama who ended up falling short. The late PBS anchor Gwen Ifill canonized Kamala Harris as “the female Barack Obama” back in 2009, but Harris’ two presidential campaigns both failed. A 2018 New Yorker article about the presidential prospects of Deval Patrick, the former Massachusetts governor, pronounced him “a kind of political heir to Barack Obama” and quoted former Obama advisers favorably comparing the two men. When Patrick did finally launch a 2020 campaign, it lasted just three months. Obama, of course, isn’t a ghost, and he has been talking up some of the same candidates others are likening to him. Obama called up El-Sayed last month and has publicly praised Talarico, New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani, and other promising young Democrats. Still, not even Obama’s Obamadar is perfect. In 2018, as Beto O’Rourke was weighing a national campaign, the former president was the one drawing comparisons between them. “The reason I was able to make a connection with a sizable portion of the country was because people had a sense that I said what I meant,” Obama said then, making clear that he felt O’Rourke shared that special touch. Former Obama aides reportedly called O’Rourke’s people to offer their help, remarking that the lanky Texan reminded them of their former boss. “He’s Barack Obama, but white,” one Democratic bundler told Politico. Judging by polling and fundraising, though, the rest of the country didn’t agree: O’Rourke ended up dropping out before a single primary vote was cast. That doesn’t mean Obama was wrong about their similarities. But it does suggest that giving party apparatchiks shivers of recognition isn’t enough. Another factor complicating things for the latest Obama mini-mes: Candidates themselves are starting to lean into the comparison. For them, giving off Obama vibes can be a convenient shortcut to national prominence, campaign contributions, and glowing press. But for the rest of us, it can be hard to tell whether someone is truly an Obama-caliber candidate or is just staring in the mirror a little too hard. El-Sayed, for example, used to shy away from the parallel. “There are a lot of things I disagree with President Obama on—in terms of both domestic and, certainly, international politics,” he told Vox during his 2018 run for Michigan governor. “I’m not him, and I have no intention of being him.” Last month, though, he struck a different tone. “I never saw myself in politics until I saw him give that 2004 speech on the DNC floor,” El-Sayed admiringly declared of the keynote address that propelled Obama to national stardom. El-Sayed has since doubled down, noting that Republicans have been highlighting his full name—Abdulrahman—just as they used to surround “Barack Hussein Obama” with scare quotes. (Trump, a leading proponent of the conspiracy theory that Obama was born in Africa, still does this.) Efforts to replicate the former president’s mien can also risk coming off like imitation—or, worse, parody. Shapiro’s cadence, inflection, and conspicuous use of the pregnant pause have long invited comparisons to Obama’s speaking style, not all of them positive. “It’s like if I tried to do a really bad impression of Barack Obama,” Vice President J.D. Vance has said. Shapiro is an electoral juggernaut—polls show him with an overwhelming lead in an otherwise purple state—so this might not matter much. But it has already become a punch line that could resurface if he runs for president. (“Barack Obama is the greatest orator of my lifetime,” Shapiro said when asked about the parallels in 2024, denying that he’d ever intentionally emulated the former president.) Seeing future standard-bearers through Obama-shaped glasses could also lead the party to some uncomfortable places. Since Trump’s return, candidates perceived as authentic, rough-hewn non-careerists have captured Democrats’ hearts. Obama sure has his charms, but he comes out of a different mold: the Harvard-educated lawyer who pictured himself in the Oval Office from an early age. Republicans seem to be realizing that an Obama redux can be spun into an insult—even as they also seem to be warily looking over their shoulders. When Fox News host Laura Ingraham declared Ossoff the Democrats’ “new Obama” in June, she did not mean it as a compliment. After a rally in which Ossoff made a knowing reference to Natalie Harp, a Trump White House aide with whom the president has a suspiciously close relationship, an administration spokesman blasted the freshman senator as “a cringeworthy, feminine theatre kid cosplaying as Barack Obama.” There are other downsides to the Obama-replication effort. Voters looking for a president often prize stereotypically masculine traits: Height, a deep voice, a healthy reverence for U.S. military might—all while still seeming chill enough to grab a beer with. Women can have all of those qualities, too, and political science research suggests female candidates often perform as well as or better than male ones. But it’s probably no coincidence that few women have cracked pundits’ list of Obama-like figures. Harris and Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, whose rapid ascent one Democratic strategist has called “eerily similar” to Obama’s, may be the only exceptions. Then, of course, there’s the small matter of actually beating Republicans in elections. Voters and consultants looking for an Obama-esque standard-bearer risk over-indexing on certain qualities—an inspiring backstory, an exciting policy agenda, or a smooth jump shot—while underrating others that may resonate beyond die-hard Democrats. As a candidate, Obama had the rare ability to come off like a centrist to centrists and like a progressive to progressives. But he also practiced strategic moderation, publicly opposing same-sex marriage during his first presidential run while both supporting a pathway to citizenship for immigrants who lacked permanent legal status and cracking down on unauthorized border crossings. In this way, superficial Obama similarities can mask differences that may end up being determinative in the voting booth. El-Sayed has thrilled progressives by supporting Medicare for All and trading insults with Trump—which may also help explain why he’s one of the swing-state Democrats most in danger of losing in November. (Most polls show El-Sayed leading his Republican opponent relatively narrowly even as Democrats in other purple states—including Ossoff, Shapiro, and Roy Cooper in North Carolina—appear comfortably ahead.) El-Sayed has also tolerated controversy to a degree that Obama never did. Confronted during the 2008 campaign with his longtime pastor Jeremiah Wright’s inflammatory comments, Obama cut Wright off, condemned his remarks “in unequivocal terms,” and delivered a speech that transubstantiated the controversy into a public meditation on racism and patriotism. El-Sayed, meanwhile, has repeatedly resisted calls—including from other Democrats—to disavow Hasan Piker, a left-wing streamer who has said that “America deserved 9/11.” (Piker has apologized for that one and El-Sayed called it a “dumb statement,” but Republicans are still trying their darnedest to turn it into a general-election liability.) There’s also the fact that campaign tactics and political mores have evolved since Obama’s day. The former president has sometimes spiced things up since leaving office, dropping the encomiums to America’s better angels he dispensed when he was on the ballot. At the 2024 Democratic National Convention, Obama suggested that Trump’s “weird obsession with crowd sizes” might have something to do with his anatomy. Still, Ossoff’s recent dig at Harp, which uncorked a geyser of speculation that she and the president may be having an affair, was a notably un-Obama-like move. David Axelrod, the former president’s onetime chief strategist, called it “gratuitous and unnecessary.” But Ossoff’s remark also commanded attention, feeding a boomlet that helped bump up his standing in polls of the hypothetical 2028 presidential primary. If the high horse Obama successfully rode into the White House is truly dead, Democrats who pretend otherwise risk getting outflanked. Last, all the Obama comparisons in the world won’t help Democrats figure out whether his technocratic, center-left brand of institutionalism remains the governing approach the party should adopt the next time it holds power in Washington. Obama himself has acknowledged that politics has changed since he left. “If I were running today, I wouldn’t run the same race or have the same platform as I did in 2008,” he said in 2020. The next Democratic congressional majority and the next Democratic president will have to make choices about which issues to prioritize and how far to push them: climate or healthcare, incremental progress or sweeping overhaul, policy or institutional change. In a sense, this is just the latest rejoinder in an ongoing fight. The 2020 Democratic primary was riven by debates over whether to build on Obama’s incremental progress on healthcare or start afresh with something like Medicare for All. That time, the pro-Obama wing won the argument, but Trump’s return has even moderate Democrats wondering if it’s time to try the other path. “It showed me the need for institutional change,” Buttigieg recently told NOTUS of his time working for Biden. “Democrats can’t take us back to the early 2020s or the 2010s,” he added. “We shouldn’t try.” Other Democrats weighing presidential runs are looking even further back in time. “I’m an FDR Democrat,” Rep. Ro Khanna of California said this week in New Hampshire. “I’m a Democrat who believes that this party needs to do big things again.” Amid all the hagiography, Obama’s record warrants some scrutiny. Some of his achievements, most notably the Affordable Care Act, still stand. But his presidency didn’t come close to solving structural issues that still plague the party today, from a gerrymandered House to a Senate tilted toward Republican-dominated states. And in Obama’s choices and temperament—from not passing a more generous economic stimulus and not pressuring Ruth Bader Ginsburg to step down from the Supreme Court to privately anointing Hillary Clinton as his preferred successor in 2016—there’s a case to be made that the 44th president bears some responsibility for the paroxysms of the Trump years. So if not the next Obama, who should Democrats be looking for? To answer that, it’s worth going back to seventh grade social studies. Even as my teacher whispered about him excitedly, Obama was far from the consensus choice. Back then, Democrats had a different broadly successful two-term president to look back on. Hillary wasn’t Bill; her 2008 campaign took pains to emphasize their differences. But she was literally another Clinton, and as late as January 2008 commanded nearly double Obama’s support among Democratic primary voters. Yet those same voters ended up rejecting a Clintonian retread—and holding the White House for the next eight years. There’s a clear lesson there. Ultimately, trying to clone Obama is likely to be a fool’s errand. He’s a pretty singular figure. So rather than fixating on candidates who resemble him in mannerisms or policy, Democrats might instead try to find someone who can do what he did: Give them a new way to think about politics that was so transformative that the party is still pining for it nearly 20 years later. As Obama memorably put it once upon a time, “Change will not come if we wait for some other person or if we wait for some other time. We are the ones we’ve been waiting for.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend
2026-09-08

Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It is a mystery why President Trump continues to rely on the hapless team of Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff—his son-in-law and fellow real-estate tycoon, respectively—to hammer out treaties to end the most destructive wars of our time: Russia vs. Ukraine and Israel vs. the Palestinians. Yes, Trump doesn’t trust the professional diplomatic corps or experts of any kind. But, as a self-proclaimed businessman, he must notice when aides don’t get the job done, and the Kushner-Witkoff duo have not lured the combatants one step closer to peace; in fact, they’ve arguably intensified the war in Ukraine. Their utter incompetence was on full display over the weekend, as they met with Vladimir Putin in Moscow, then—in their first trip to the Ukrainian capital—Volodymyr Zelensky in Kyiv. They carried what Trump touted ahead of time as new peace proposals, and concluded both sets of talks with boilerplate adjectives (“productive,” “meaningful,” “positive,” “businesslike”). But in fact, they came away with zilch—and, once again, proved themselves far short of the task. This should come as no surprise. Neither has any diplomatic experience; neither knows anything about Russia or Ukraine or military strategy; nor do they travel with any specialists who might advise them along the way. Witkoff kicked off the tour with an act of full-bore genuflection toward Putin, greeting the Kremlin dictator as follows: First, Mr. President, thank you so much for having us. We were sitting outside just now, me, Jared, and Kirill [Dmitriev], and then Yuri [Ushakov] joined us, and we were all talking about when we retire, we’ll have all these incredible stories and incredible memories, and yours will be right at the top of it all. In other words, Mr. President, he might have added, we come to praise you, not pressure you into taking any actions that you don’t feel like taking. Later in the day, the aforementioned Ushakov, Putin’s senior diplomat, released a summary of the Kremlin team’s position at the meeting: The Russian army has had battlefield successes, meaning it might still win the war; Ukrainian troops must withdraw from the eastern Donbas regions; the war’s “root cause” is Zelensky’s belief that Ukraine is an independent nation; and peace will bring many business opportunities between Russia and the United States. In other words, the usual propaganda. (The U.S. envoys did not release an official “readout” of the meeting, perhaps because they are not officials. That’s right: Kushner and Witkoff hold no government posts, have not been confirmed by the Senate, and are unaccountable to anyone but Trump.) The duo then flew to Poland and from there proceeded to Kyiv by train. (Zelensky had promised not to bomb Moscow while the two were in the Kremlin, but Putin offered no such pledge in return, so the U.S. security team felt Kyiv’s airport was unsafe. In any case, Putin resumed bombarding Ukraine’s capital city shortly after the two departed. Such a good friend.) After this meeting, Zelensky insisted that the three hold a press conference, mainly with Ukrainian reporters. It did not go well. Asked if he felt “ashamed” to meet at the same table with an international war criminal like Putin, Witkoff replied: We’ve always been treated respectfully by President Putin and his team. Relationships are critical in terms of solving conflicts like this. My job, and Jared’s job, is to bring the parties together. All three sentences reflect, and reaffirm, Witkoff’s utter naivete. They have not been treated respectfully; hence the refusal to issue a hands-off order on Kyiv’s airport while the two were in town. Relationships are not critical in solving conflicts; managing interests, either through diplomacy or force, is key. (Trump, too, doesn’t understand this.) Finally, if Witkoff thinks his job is simply to bring the parties together, he doesn’t understand his job. Kushner repeated much of Witkoff’s nonsense, saying that the point of negotiations is to produce a “win-win,” including some sort of favor that fulfills Russia’s “aspirations.” Since Russia’s chief aspirations—as Putin himself has declared—are to obliterate Ukraine as an independent nation and to restore much of the old Russian Empire, this isn’t likely to be the basis for an equitable peace. Along the same lines, Kushner expressed hope that the U.S., Russia, and Ukraine could resume the trilateral talks that began shortly before Trump got sidetracked by the war on Iran (though Kushner didn’t express the chronology that way). The few talks that took place were, as he recalled, such jolly affairs. “Everyone was speaking in Russian to each other. We were discussing these issues in a very extra-friendly manner, all in Russian.” If he’d been escorted by a specialist, Kushner might have been informed that speaking Russian doesn’t make Ukrainians feel merry and welcome, especially with Russian officials who want to be their overlords. (Many Ukrainians these days regard the Russian language, including classic Russian literature, as inherently imperialist.) Another Kushner faux pas, this one perhaps reflecting his ignorance of European history: “There is no such thing in the world as forever enemies or forever allies.” He said this in the context of urging Ukrainians to imagine that they might once again be friendly with Russians. However, the analogy that some observers thought he was invoking—the U.S. alliance with Germany after World War II—was not at all fitting, as it developed only after the U.S. and other allies defeated Nazi Germany, deposed its government, tried many of its leaders for war crimes, and imposed a new constitution. Even then, it wasn’t until 10 years after the war ended that the new Federal Republic of Germany was trusted enough for the allies to invite it to join NATO. Clearly, Kushner wasn’t proposing that Putin be killed or that his government be transformed—though many in the audience might have hoped that he would. It has never been entirely clear how Trump would like this war to end—or if he thinks it should end, or whether he cares one way or the other. Until he decides, keeping Kushner and Witkoff on the case is a waste of jet fuel and time. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
